## F001

**Question:** In Figure 1B-1, what happens immediately after FHWA approves a request to experiment?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1B.05, Figure 1B-1, page 7, p.7, p.8, p.9. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The requesting jurisdiction installs the experimental traffic control device. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=1.000. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=0.00, visual recall=0.00, and visual precision=0.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: According to Figure 1B-1, once FHWA approves the request (the "Yes" branch of the "Approved?" decision), the next step is that the requesting jurisdiction installs the experimental traffic control device. From there the process splits into two parallel activities—evaluating the experimental device and providing semi-annual reports to the FHWA Division & HQ—both of which lead to the jurisdiction providing FHWA a copy of the final report. Standards (mandatory): - "The required semi-annual progress reports shall be submitted throughout the course of an approved experiment in accordance with the following schedule: A. No later than August 1st for the preceding period of January through June; and B. No later than February 1st for the preceding period of July through December." - "The experimenting agency shall submit a semi-annual progress report for any approved experiment even if no work was performed during the previous reporting period. Failure to submit two consecutive progress reports shall result in termination of the experiment and shall constitute rescission of the FHWA's approval to the experimenting agency, requiring restoration of the site(s) to a condition that complies with the provisions of this Manual within 3 months." - "The experimenting agency shall submit a final report within 3 months of the conclusion of an approved experiment. If a final report is not received by the FHWA's Office of Transportation Operations, and the experimenting agency fails to notify the FHWA of any mitigating circumstances within 6 months of the end of the approved experimentation period, then the experiment shall be considered terminated and shall constitute rescission of the FHWA's approval to the experimenting agency, requiring restoration of the site(s) to a condition that complies with the provisions of this Manual within 3 months." Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Figure 1B-1, where the "Yes" arrow from the "Approved?" diamond leads directly to the box "Requesting jurisdiction installs experimental traffic control device," which then branches to "Evaluate experimental traffic control device" and "Requesting jurisdiction provides semi-annual reports to FHWA Division & HQ," both converging on "Requesting jurisdiction provides FHWA a copy of final report." [Image 2] shows the same Figure 1B-1 flowchart in full-page context on page 7, confirming the sequence following the "Yes" branch of the approval decision. Citations: - Figure 1B-1 (p.7) - Section 1B.05 Standard §12 (p.8) - Section 1B.05 Standard §13 (p.8) - Section 1B.05 Standard §14 (p.9)

**Gold answer:** The requesting jurisdiction installs the experimental traffic control device.

## F002

**Question:** In Figure 1B-2, if a proposed change is accepted for Federal rulemaking, what process follows before a Final Rule is published?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1B.02, Figure 1B-2, Page 10, p.10, p.4, 23 CFR 655.603(b). Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: FHWA develops provisions for a Notice of Proposed Amendments, publishes the notice in the Federal Register, holds a docket comment period, reviews comments, prepares the Final Rul… Retrieval context: The top five text chunks did not include a chunk that matched the locked page, section, table, or figure evidence. No relevant text chunk appeared in the six logged ranks, so MRR was 0. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.000 and Context Precision@6=0.000. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: According to Figure 1B-2, once a proposed change is accepted for Federal rulemaking, FHWA develops device provisions for inclusion in the next Notice of Proposed Amendments, publishes that Notice in the Federal Register, holds a docket comment period, reviews the comments received, and then prepares the Final Rule before publishing it. In parallel, FHWA may consider interim approval, allowing jurisdictions to apply for, receive, and deploy the device under interim approval conditions while rulemaking proceeds. After the Final Rule is published, State manuals must be brought into substantial conformance with the National MUTCD within 2 years. Standards (mandatory): - "States or other Federal agencies that have their own MUTCDs or Supplements shall revise these MUTCDs or Supplements to be in substantial conformance with changes to the National MUTCD within 2 years of the effective date of the Final Rule for the changes [23 CFR 655.603(b)(3)]. Substantial conformance of such State or other Federal agency MUTCDs or Supplements shall be as defined in 23 CFR 655.603(b)(1)." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 1B-2 shows the "YES" branch from the "Accepted for Federal rulemaking?" decision leading through the sequence: FHWA develops device provisions for the next Notice of Proposed Amendments → FHWA publishes Notice of Proposed Amendments in the Federal Register → Docket comment period → FHWA reviews comments → FHWA prepares Final Rule → FHWA publishes Final Rule, ending with the box "State manuals must be in substantial conformance with the National MUTCD within 2 years as specified in 23 CFR 655.603(a)"; it also shows the parallel "Interim approval?" branch in which jurisdictions may deploy devices under interim approval conditions pending the Final Rule. [Image 2] shows the same Figure 1B-2 flowchart in full-page context on Page 10, confirming the identical rulemaking sequence. Citations: - Figure 1B-2 (p.10) - Section 1B.02 Standard §2 (p.4)

**Gold answer:** FHWA develops provisions for a Notice of Proposed Amendments, publishes the notice in the Federal Register, holds a docket comment period, reviews comments, prepares the Final Rule, and publishes it.

Citations: - Figure 2A-1 (p.48) - Section 2A.11 Option §1 (p.49) - Section 2A.11 Standard §4 (p.49)

## F004

**Question:** In Figure 2A-2, what minimum mounting height and lateral offset are shown for a basic roadside sign in a rural area without a wider shoulder?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2A.13, Section 2A.16, Section 2A.15, Figure 2A-2, Figure 2A-3, p.58. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: A 5-foot minimum mounting height and a 12-foot minimum lateral offset from the edge of the traveled way. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.500. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Panel A of Figure 2A-2 ("Roadside sign in rural area") shows a minimum mounting height of 5 feet, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the traveled way, and a minimum lateral offset of 12 feet from the edge of the traveled way. The 6-foot offset (Panel B) applies only where a shoulder wider than 6 feet exists, so without such a shoulder the 12-foot dimension governs. Standards (mandatory): - "In rural areas, the minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement, of signs installed at the side of the road shall be 5 feet (see Figure 2A-2)." Guidance (recommended): - The figure note text accompanying Section 2A.13 explains that lateral offset is a minimum of 6 feet measured from the edge of the shoulder, or 12 feet measured from the edge of the traveled way, with Section 2A.16 providing reduced lateral offset distances where offsets are limited. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2A-2, Panel A shows a rural roadside STOP sign with "12 ft MIN." lateral offset from the edge of the traveled way and "5 ft MIN." mounting height, while Panel B shows the alternative "6 ft MIN." offset that applies only where the shoulder is wider than 6 feet. - [Image 2] The full-page view of Figure 2A-2 confirms the same Panel A dimensions (12 ft MIN. offset, 5 ft MIN. height) for a roadside sign in a rural area. - [Image 5] Figure 2A-3's note reiterates that lateral offset is a minimum of 6 feet measured from the edge of the shoulder, or 12 feet measured from the edge of the traveled way. Citations: - Section 2A.15 Standard §4 (p.58) - Section 2A.13 Guidance §6 (p.57) - Figure 2A-2 (p.51) - Page 92 - Page 93

**Gold answer:** A 5-foot minimum mounting height and a 12-foot minimum lateral offset from the edge of the traveled way.

The response identifies: Section 2A.23, Section 2B.50, Section 2B.49, Figure 2B-13, Figure 2B-19, Figure 2A-5. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: In the separate-intersections case, the paths of opposing left-turning vehicles cross Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.500. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The distinguishing visual feature is whether the paths of opposing left-turning vehicles cross each other in the median area. In the "A – Separate intersections" panel, the median is wide and the two blue left-turn paths cross each other within the median opening; in the "B – Single intersection" panel, the median is narrow and the opposing left-turn paths curve inside one another without crossing. Standards (mandatory): - "On unsignalized minor-street approaches from which both left turns and right turns are permitted onto a divided highway at a crossing that functions as two separate intersections (see Section 2A.23), except as provided in Paragraph 2 of this Section, a Divided Highway Crossing (R6-3 or R6-3a) sign (see Figure 2B-13) shall be used to advise road users that they are approaching an intersection with a divided highway (see Figure 2B-19)." - "At the crossing of a roadway with a divided highway that functions as two separate intersections, ONE WAY signs shall be placed, visible to each crossroad approach, on the near right and far left corners of each intersection with the directional roadways (see Figure 2B-19)." Guidance (recommended): - A divided highway crossing should be signed and marked as separate intersections when both of the following conditions are present: (A) the paths of opposing left turns from the divided highway cross each other (see Figure 2A-5), and (B) there is adequate storage in the interior approaches for the design vehicles expected to cross the divided highway. - At the crossing of two divided highways, engineering judgment should be used to determine the number of separate intersections. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2A-5 shows panel A labeled "Separate intersections (paths of opposing left-turning vehicles cross)," where the blue left-turn path arrows visibly intersect within a wide median, versus panel B labeled "Single intersection (paths of opposing left-turning vehicles do not cross)," where the narrow median forces the left-turn paths to curve around each other without crossing. - [Image 2] Page 105 (full page view of Figure 2A-5) confirms the same contrast: the wide-median layout in panel A produces crossing left-turn paths, while the narrow-median layout in panel B produces non-crossing paths. - [Image 3] Page 93 shows Figure 2A-3 (sign placement at intersections) and does not bear on this distinction. - [Image 4] Page 1145 shows Figure 9E-10 (two-stage bicycle turn boxes) and does not bear on this distinction. - [Image 5] Page 588 shows Figure 3B-7 (two-way left-turn lane markings) and does not bear on this distinction. Citations: - Section 2A.23 Guidance §1 (p.63) - Section 2A.23 Guidance §3 (p.64) - Section 2B.50 Standard §1 (p.116) - Section 2B.49 Standard §3 (p.114) - Figure 2A-5 (p.64) - Page 105

**Gold answer:** In the separate-intersections case, the paths of opposing left-turning vehicles cross; in the single-intersection case, those paths do not cross.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: In Figure 2B-1, the STOP (R1-1) sign is an octagon (red background with white legend and border), and the YIELD (R1-2) sign is a downward-pointing (inverted) equilateral triangle with a red border and white interior. The ALL-WAY (R1-3P) plaque has a white legend and border on a red background. Standards (mandatory): - "At intersections where all approaches are controlled by STOP signs (see Section 2B.12), an ALL-WAY (R1-3P) supplemental plaque (see Figure 2B-1) shall be mounted below each STOP sign. The ALL-WAY plaque shall have a white legend and border on a red background." - "When it is determined that a full stop is always required on an approach to an intersection, a STOP (R1-1) sign (see Figure 2B-1) shall be used." - "The YIELD (R1-2) sign (see Figure 2B-1) shall not be displayed using a changeable message sign." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2B-1 shows the R1-1 STOP sign as a red octagon with white legend and border, the R1-2 YIELD sign as a red downward-pointing triangle with a white interior containing the red word "YIELD," and the R1-3P ALL-WAY plaque as a horizontal red rectangle with white legend and border. - [Image 2] The full page containing Section 2B.04 and Figure 2B-1 confirms the octagonal STOP sign, triangular YIELD sign, and the red ALL-WAY plaque with white legend and border. - [Image 3] This page (Figure 7B-1) shows the same octagonal STOP and triangular YIELD symbols reproduced within school-crossing signs, consistent with the shapes in Figure 2B-1. - [Image 4] This page (Figure 6G-1) shows the R1-1 octagonal STOP sign and R1-2 triangular YIELD sign used in temporary traffic control zones, matching the Figure 2B-1 shapes. - [Image 5] This page (Figure 2B-20) shows octagonal STOP signs in intersection signing layouts, consistent with the R1-1 shape in Figure 2B-1. Citations: - Section 2B.04 Standard §4 (p.74) - Section 2B.04 Standard §1 (p.74) - Section 2B.05 Standard §2 (p.75) - Figure 2B-1 (p.74)

**Gold answer:** STOP is an octagon; YIELD is a downward-pointing equilateral triangle; the ALL-WAY plaque has white legend and border on a red background.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Figure 4D-2 shows that the primary signal faces must be located no less than 40 feet beyond the stop line and no more than 180 feet beyond it (the 180-foot maximum applies to 12-inch signal faces and may only be exceeded if a near-side supplemental signal face is provided). The figure also shows a shorter maximum of 120 feet from the stop line for 8-inch signal faces. Standards (mandatory): - "A signal face installed to satisfy the requirements for primary left-turn signal faces (see Sections 4F.02 through 4F.08) and primary right-turn signal faces (see Sections 4F.09 through 4F.15), and at least one and preferably both of the minimum of two primary signal faces required for the through movement (or the major turning movement if there is no through movement) on the approach shall be located: 1. No less than 40 feet beyond the stop line, and 2. No more than 180 feet beyond the stop line unless a supplemental near-side signal face is provided." - "The primary signal faces that are used to satisfy the requirements of Item A shall simultaneously satisfy the lateral placement requirement described in Section 4D.07 (see Figure 4D-2)." - The same faces "shall be located between two lines intersecting with the center of the approach at a point 10 feet behind the stop line, one making an angle of approximately 20 degrees to the right of the center of the approach extended, and the other making an angle of approximately 20 degrees to the left of the center of the approach extended." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 4D-2 annotates "40 ft*" as the "Minimum distance of signal faces from stop line," "120 ft**" as the "Maximum distance from stop line for 8-inch signal face," and "180 ft***" as the "Maximum distance from stop line for 12-inch signal faces, unless a near-side supplemental signal face is used," within a wedge bounded by 20° lines from a point 10 ft behind the stop line. - [Image 2] Page 713 shows the complete Figure 4D-2 page with the same dimensional annotations and footnotes. - [Image 3] Page 714 contains Section 4D.08, which codifies the 40-foot minimum and 180-foot maximum longitudinal distances from the stop line. - [Image 4] Page 712 contains Section 4D.07, establishing the 20-degree lateral placement wedge measured from 10 feet behind the stop line that must be satisfied simultaneously. - [Image 5] Page 709 (Section 4D.05) provides related provisions on the number of signal faces referenced in Figure 4D-2's notes for higher-speed approaches. Citations: - Section 4D.08 Standard §1 (p.673) - Section 4D.07 Standard §1 (p.671) - Figure 4D-2 (p.672) - Page 713 - Page 714 - Page 712

**Gold answer:** At least 40 feet and no more than 180 feet beyond the stop line.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Figure 4I-4 shows the pedestrian intervals in the following order: the Walk Interval (steady WALKING PERSON), followed by the Pedestrian Change Interval (flashing UPRAISED HAND with countdown), followed by the Buffer Interval (steady UPRAISED HAND). The first interval (Walk Interval) is labeled "7 seconds MIN.," and the last interval (Buffer Interval) is labeled "2 seconds MIN." Standards (mandatory): - "A pedestrian change interval consisting of a flashing UPRAISED HAND (symbolizing DONT WALK) signal indication shall begin immediately following the WALKING PERSON (symbolizing WALK) signal indication. Following the pedestrian change interval, a buffer interval consisting of a steady UPRAISED HAND (symbolizing DONT WALK) signal indication shall be displayed for at least 2 seconds prior to the release of any conflicting vehicular movement." - "The sum of the time of the pedestrian change interval and the buffer interval shall not be less than the calculated pedestrian clearance time (see Paragraphs 7 through 16 of this Section). The buffer interval shall not begin later than the beginning of the red clearance interval, if used." - "All pedestrian signal heads used at crosswalks where the pedestrian change interval is more than 7 seconds shall include a pedestrian change interval countdown display in order to inform pedestrians of the number of seconds remaining in the pedestrian change interval." Options (permitted): - Per the figure notes: "The countdown display is optional for Pedestrian Change Intervals of 7 seconds or less"; "The Walk Interval may be reduced under some conditions (see Section 4I.06)"; and "The Buffer Interval, which shall always be provided and displayed, may be used to help satisfy the calculated pedestrian clearance time, or may begin after the calculated pedestrian clearance time has ended." - "The additional time provided by an extended push button press to satisfy pedestrian clearance time needs may be added to either the walk interval or the pedestrian change interval." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 4I-4 shows the sequence Walk Interval → Pedestrian Change Interval → Buffer Interval, with the Walk Interval labeled "7 seconds MIN.**", the Buffer Interval labeled "2 seconds MIN.", the calculated pedestrian clearance time spanning the change interval (and optionally the buffer), and the "Zero" point of the countdown display marked at the end of the pedestrian change interval. - [Image 2] Page 765 reproduces Figure 4I-4 in the context of Section 4I.06, showing the same interval order and the 7-second and 2-second minimum labels along with the relationships to associated vehicular phase intervals (G, Y, R). - [Image 3] Page 764 contains Figure 4I-3 (push button locations) and the Standard text of Section 4I.06 §4 requiring the change interval to follow the WALK indication and the 2-second minimum buffer interval. - [Image 4] Page 767 contains related Guidance and Support on walk interval timing at leading pedestrian interval locations but does not depict the figure itself. - [Image 5] Page 37 is a table of contents page listing "Figure 4I-4 Pedestrian Intervals" on page 724. Citations: - Figure 4I-4 (p.724) - Section 4I.06 Standard §4 (p.724) - Section 4I.04 Standard §1 (p.720) - Section 4I.06 Option §9 (p.725) - Page 765 - Page 764

**Gold answer:** Walk interval, pedestrian change interval, then buffer interval. The walk interval is labeled 7 seconds minimum and the buffer interval 2 seconds minimum, subject to the stated walk-interval exception.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Figure 4J-3 shows the pedestrian hybrid beacon sequence as six states: (1) Dark Until Activated; (2) Flashing Yellow Upon Activation; (3) Steady Yellow; (4) Steady Red During Pedestrian Walk Interval; (5) Alternating Flashing Red During Pedestrian Change Interval; and (6) Dark Again Until Activated. This sequence is mandated by the operational Standard in Section 4J.03. Standards (mandatory): - "Following an actuation by a pedestrian, a pedestrian hybrid beacon face shall display a flashing CIRCULAR yellow signal indication, followed by a steady CIRCULAR yellow signal indication, followed by both steady CIRCULAR RED signal indications during the pedestrian walk interval, followed by alternating flashing CIRCULAR RED signal indications during the pedestrian change interval (see Figure 4J-3). Upon ... termination of the pedestrian change interval, the pedestrian hybrid beacon faces shall revert to a dark (not illuminated) condition." - "A pedestrian hybrid beacon face shall consist of three signal sections, with a CIRCULAR YELLOW signal indication centered below two horizontally-aligned CIRCULAR RED signal indications (see Figure 4J-3)." Options (permitted): - Per the note in Figure 4J-3, "An optional steady red clearance interval may be added after Interval 3, and an optional short buffer interval (alternating flashing red while the pedestrian signal heads are displaying a steady UPRAISED HAND) may be added after Interval 5." Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Figure 4J-3 with the six labeled states — 1. Dark Until Activated, 2. Flashing Yellow Upon Activation, 3. Steady Yellow, 4. Steady Red During Pedestrian Walk Interval, 5. Alternating Flashing Red During Pedestrian Change Interval, and 6. Dark Again Until Activated — plus a legend (SY, FY, SR, FR) and the note on optional intervals. [Image 3] (page 770) shows the same Figure 4J-3 in the Revision 1 page layout, confirming the six-state sequence. Citations: - Figure 4J-3 (p.729) - Section 4J.03 Standard §2 (p.730) - Section 4J.02 Standard §2 (p.728) - Page 770

**Gold answer:** Dark until activated; flashing yellow upon activation; steady yellow; steady red during the pedestrian walk interval; alternating flashing red during the pedestrian change interval; dark again until activated.

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1D.03, Section 1C.01, p.13. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: It is recommended practice, not a mandatory Standard. | A deviation may be appropriate when engineering judgment or an engineering study indicates that the deviation is suitable for the specific situation. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No, a Guidance provision is not mandatory. Guidance is a statement of recommended practice in typical situations, and a deviation is appropriate when engineering judgment or an engineering study indicates that the deviation is appropriate. Standards (mandatory): - "Guidance-a statement of recommended practice in typical situations, with deviations allowed if engineering judgment or engineering study (see Section 1D.03) indicates the deviation to be appropriate. All Guidance statements are labeled, and the text appears in unbold italic type. The verb 'should' is typically used. The verbs 'shall' and 'may' are not used in Guidance statements. Guidance statements are sometimes modified by Option statements." - By contrast, a Standard is "a statement of required, mandatory, or specifically prohibitive practice regarding a traffic control device," where "the verb 'shall' is typically used" — confirming that only Standard statements, not Guidance statements, carry mandatory force. Citations: - Section 1C.01 Standard §1 (p.13)

**Gold answer:** It is recommended practice, not a mandatory Standard. A deviation may be appropriate when engineering judgment or an engineering study indicates that the deviation is suitable for the specific situation.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A vertical retroreflective strip on a sign support is one of the permitted methods for enhancing sign conspicuity, chosen based on engineering judgment. If used, the strip must be at least 2 inches wide, run the full length of the support from the sign down to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway, match the background color of the sign (except red for YIELD and DO NOT ENTER signs), and carry no legend or other information. Standards (mandatory): - "If a strip of retroreflective material is used on the sign support, it shall be at least 2 inches in width, it shall be placed for the full length of the support from the sign to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway, and its color shall match the background color of the sign, except that the color of the strip for the YIELD and DO NOT ENTER signs shall be red. The retroreflective strip shall not display any legend or other information." Options (permitted): - Based upon engineering judgment, where improvement of the conspicuity of a standard regulatory, warning, or guide sign is desired, any of the methods illustrated in Figure 2A-1 — including a vertical retroreflective strip on the sign support — may be used, as appropriate. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2A-1, Example E, shows a fluorescent yellow-green pedestrian warning sign assembly with a matching-color vertical retroreflective strip extending down the support, with the strip's lower end dimensioned "2 ft MAX." above the near edge of the roadway. - [Image 2] The full-page view of Figure 2A-1 confirms the strip's color matches the sign background and shows the 2-foot maximum gap measured to the elevation of the near edge of the roadway. - [Image 4] This page presents the Standard text confirming strip width (at least 2 inches), full-length placement, color matching, and the prohibition on displaying any legend or other information. - [Image 5] A grade-crossing figure similarly depicts 2-inch retroreflective strips on a sign support with a "2 ft MAX." dimension measured to the elevation of the near edge of the roadway, consistent with the retroreflective-strip concept. Citations: - Section 2A.11 Standard §5 (p.49) - Section 2A.11 Option §1 (p.49) - Figure 2A-1 (p.48) - Page 90 - Page 1038

**Gold answer:** The strip must be at least 2 inches wide, extend from the sign down the full support to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway, match the sign background color except that YIELD and DO NOT ENTER strips are red, and contain no legend or other information.

Figure 2A-2 (Panels A and B) shows the normal lateral offset as a minimum of 12 feet from the edge of the traveled way, or a minimum of 6 feet from the edge of the shoulder where the shoulder is wider than 6 feet; Section 2A.16 addresses lateral offsets, including the 6-foot minimum for overhead sign supports and reduced offsets in constrained areas. Standards (mandatory): - "In rural areas, the minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement, of signs installed at the side of the road shall be 5 feet (see Figure 2A-2)." - For comparison in developed areas: "In business, commercial, or residential areas where parking, bicyclist, or pedestrian movements are likely to occur, or where the view of the sign might be obstructed, the minimum height... of signs installed at the side of the road shall be 7 feet (see Figure 2A-2)." - For overhead supports (the offset provision quoted in the available evidence): "For overhead sign supports, the minimum lateral offset from the edge of the shoulder (or if no shoulder exists, from the edge of the pavement) to the near edge of overhead sign supports (cantilever or sign bridges) shall be 6 feet." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2A-2, Panel A ("Roadside sign in rural area") shows a STOP sign with "12 ft MIN." lateral offset from the edge of the traveled way and "5 ft MIN." mounting height, while Panel B shows "6 ft MIN." offset from the edge of a shoulder wider than 6 feet with the same "5 ft MIN." height. - [Image 2] The full page containing Figure 2A-2 confirms these dimensions and notes that Section 2A.16 allows reduced lateral offsets where offsets are limited. - [Image 3] Figure 2M-3 (recreational/cultural signs) repeats the same rural pattern: "12 ft MIN." from the edge of the traveled way (or "6 ft MIN." from a paved shoulder) with "5 ft MIN." height. - [Image 4] Figure 2A-3's note states: "Lateral offset is a minimum of 6 feet measured from the edge of the shoulder, or 12 feet measured from the edge of the traveled way," confirming the normal roadside offset values. - [Image 5] The page containing Sections 2A.15/2A.16 shows the mounting height standards and the beginning of the Lateral Offset section. **Gold answer:** The minimum mounting height is 5 feet. The normal minimum lateral offset is 12 feet from the edge of the traveled way, or 6 feet from the edge of a shoulder wider than 6 feet.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No, "DLY" is not acceptable. Table 1D-3 lists DLY as an unacceptable abbreviation for "Delay" because road users commonly misinterpret it as "Daily," and Section 1D.08 makes clear that abbreviations in Table 1D-3 shall not be used on any traffic control device, including portable CMS. Standards (mandatory): - "The abbreviations shown in Table 1D-3 shall not be used in connection with traffic control devices because of their potential to be misinterpreted by road users." - "When the word messages shown in Table 1D-2 need to be abbreviated on a portable changeable message sign, the abbreviations shown in Table 1D-2 shall be used. Unless indicated by an asterisk, these abbreviations shall only be used on portable changeable message signs." - "Abbreviations displayed on CMS shall comply with the provisions of Section 1D.08." Guidance (recommended): - The abbreviations for words listed in Table 1D-2 that show a prompt word should not be used on a portable changeable message sign unless the prompt word shown in Table 1D-2 either precedes or follows the abbreviation, as applicable — reinforcing that only sanctioned abbreviations with proper context should appear on a CMS. - If Table 1D-1 or 1D-2 allows more than one abbreviation for a word or phrase, the same abbreviation should be used throughout a single jurisdiction. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Table 1D-3 explicitly lists "DLY" with intended word "Delay" and common misinterpretation "Daily," confirming it is an unacceptable abbreviation. [Image 2] Table 1D-2 (Sheet 1 of 2) lists the abbreviations that shall be used on portable CMS, and "DLY"/"Delay" does not appear as an approved entry. [Image 3] Page 37 shows Table 1D-3 in full context, with DLY → Delay/Daily among the prohibited abbreviations. [Image 5] Page 34 shows Section 1D.08, including the Standard that Table 1D-3 abbreviations shall not be used because of their potential misinterpretation. [Image 6] Page 516 shows Section 2L.05's Standard that abbreviations displayed on CMS shall comply with Section 1D.08. Citations: - Section 1D.08 Standard §4 (p.34) - Section 1D.08 Standard §2 (p.34) - Section 2L.05 Standard §6 (p.516) - Section 1D.08 Guidance §3 (p.34) - Section 1D.08 Guidance §5 (p.34) - Table 1D-3 (p.37) - Table 1D-2 (p.36)

**Gold answer:** No. DLY is explicitly prohibited because it can be misinterpreted as DAILY.

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The agency complies by having and using a method designed to maintain the minimums, even if some individual signs fall below them at a particular moment. | For white on red, the table gives white at least 35, red at least 7 cd/lx/m², and at least a 3:1 white-to-red contrast ratio. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.944 and Context Precision@6=0.667. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. Per the explanatory (Support) text in Section 2A.22, compliance with the Standard is achieved by having an assessment or management method in place and using it — "an agency or official having jurisdiction would be in compliance with the Standard in Paragraph 2 of this Section even if there are some individual signs that do not meet the minimum retroreflectivity levels at a particular point in time." For white-on-red signs such as STOP signs, Table 2A-5 sets minimum maintained levels of W ≥ 35 and R ≥ 7 (all sheeting types), with a minimum sign contrast ratio of at least 3:1 (white retroreflectivity ÷ red retroreflectivity). Standards (mandatory): - "Public agencies or officials having jurisdiction shall use an assessment or management method that is designed to maintain sign retroreflectivity at or above the minimum levels in Table 2A-5." (Note: the mandate attaches to using the method, not to every individual sign's condition at every moment.) - "Regulatory, warning, and guide signs (see Section 2A.03), and object markers, shall be retroreflective or illuminated to show the same shape and similar color by both day and night, unless otherwise provided in this Manual for a particular sign or group of signs." - "Regulatory signs shall be retroreflective or illuminated (see Section 2A.21)." — STOP signs are regulatory signs. Guidance (recommended): - One or more of the methods described in "Maintaining Traffic Sign Retroreflectivity" (FHWA-SA-07-020, Revised 2013), or a method developed based on an engineering study, should be used to maintain sign retroreflectivity at or above the minimum levels in Table 2A-5; signs identified through the agency's method as being below the minimum levels should be replaced. Options (permitted): - Highway agencies may exclude certain signs from the retroreflectivity maintenance guidelines: Parking, Standing, and Stopping (R7 and R8 series) signs; Walking/Hitchhiking/Crossing (R9 series, R10-1 through R10-4b) signs; Acknowledgment signs; and Bikeway signs intended for exclusive use by bicyclists or pedestrians. (STOP signs are not among the permitted exclusions.) Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Table 2A-5, "Minimum Maintained Retroreflectivity Levels," including the white-on-red row (W ≥ 35; R ≥ 7) and the minimum contrast-ratio criterion (≥ 3:1) applicable to STOP-type signs. [Image 2] shows the full page containing Section 2A.22, including the Support paragraph stating that an agency using an assessment or management method remains in compliance even if some individual signs are below the minimum levels at a particular point in time. Citations: - Section 2A.22 Standard §2 (p.62) - Section 2A.22 Guidance §4 (p.62) - Section 2A.22 Option §5 (p.63) - Section 2A.21 Standard §3 (p.62) - Section 2B.01 Standard §3 (p.67) - Table 2A-5 (p.62)

**Gold answer:** Not automatically. The agency complies by having and using a method designed to maintain the minimums, even if some individual signs fall below them at a particular moment. For white on red, the table gives white at least 35, red at least 7 cd/lx/m², and at least a 3:1 white-to-red contrast ratio.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. Under the general sign-dimension Standard, sizes smaller than those in the Conventional Road columns of the sign size tables may only be used on low-speed roadways, alleys, site roadways open to public travel, or low-volume rural roads with operating speeds of 30 mph or less — so a 35 mph multi-lane roadway does not qualify for a reduced size, and the prescribed (full) table size must be used. However, the evidence provided does not include the regulatory sign size table (e.g., Table 2B-1) that specifies the exact required STOP sign dimensions for multi-lane approaches, so the specific STOP sign size cannot be stated from this evidence. Standards (mandatory): - "The sign dimensions prescribed in the sign size tables in the various Parts and Chapters in this Manual and in the 'Standard Highway Signs' publication (see Section 1A.05) shall be used unless engineering judgment determines that other sizes are appropriate in accordance with the following. Except as provided in Paragraph 3 of this Section, where engineering judgment determines that sizes smaller than the prescribed dimensions are appropriate for use, the sign dimensions shall not be less than the minimum dimensions specified in this Manual." - "The sizes shown in the Minimum columns that are smaller than the sizes shown in the Conventional Road columns in the various sign size tables in this Manual shall only be used on low-speed roadways, alleys, site roadways open to public travel, and on low-volume rural roads with operating speeds of 30 mph or less; and only where the reduced legend size would be adequate for the regulation or warning or where physical conditions preclude the use of larger sizes." Visual evidence: None of the attached images ([Image 1]–[Image 8]) contains the regulatory sign size table needed to state the specific STOP sign dimension for a multi-lane roadway. Citations: - Section 2A.07 Standard §2 (p.45) Note on insufficiency: The provided evidence establishes that reduced/minimum sign sizes are prohibited at 35 mph, but it does not include the specific size table entry for the STOP (R1-1) sign on multi-lane roadways, so the exact required STOP sign size cannot be quoted from this evidence.

**Gold answer:** For regulatory signs other than STOP, the single-lane size may be used at 35 mph or less. The STOP-sign exception remains: the multi-lane STOP size is 36 x 36 inches.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: For a 55-mph posted or 85th-percentile speed, Table 2C-3 shows an advance placement distance of 495 feet under Condition B at 0 mph — the "potential stop" condition (footnote 3), which applies to Stop Ahead, Yield Ahead, Signal Ahead, and Intersection Warning signs. No — the table is expressly titled "Guidelines," and the accompanying Guidance in Section 2C.04 indicates the distances should be applied with engineering judgment and periodically evaluated, so 495 feet is a recommended value, not an inflexible mandate. What is mandatory is that an advance traffic control sign be installed where the primary device is not sufficiently visible, with Table 2C-3 used as the placement reference. Standards (mandatory): - "The Stop Ahead (W3-1), Yield Ahead (W3-2), and Signal Ahead (W3-3) Advance Traffic Control signs (see Figure 2C-8) shall be installed on an approach to a primary traffic control device that is not visible for a sufficient distance to permit the road user to respond to the device (see Table 2C-3)." - At passive grade crossings with a YIELD or STOP sign: "The Yield Ahead or Stop Ahead sign shall be located in accordance with Table 2C-3. The minimum distance between the signs shall be in accordance with Section 2C.04 and Table 2C-3." Guidance (recommended): - Table 2C-3 is presented as "Guidelines for Advance Placement of Warning Signs," and Section 2C.04 recommends that "the effectiveness of the placement of warning signs should be periodically evaluated under both day and night conditions" — reinforcing that the tabulated distances are recommended values to be applied with engineering judgment rather than fixed requirements. Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Table 2C-3, where the 55-mph row under the Condition B "0³" column reads 495 ft, and footnote 3 explains that this column represents the potential stop condition (Stop Ahead, Yield Ahead, Signal Ahead, and Intersection Warning signs) based on 2018 AASHTO stopping sight distance with a 2.5-second PRT and 11.2 ft/s² deceleration. [Image 2] shows the full Section 2C.04 page, where the Guidance statements state that warning signs "should be placed so that they provide an adequate PRT" and that "the distances contained in Table 2C-3 should be applied with engineering judgment," confirming the distances are recommendations. [Image 3] and [Image 5] show example sign-sequence figures that direct the reader to "See Table 2C-3 to determine the advance placement distance," illustrating the table's role as a placement guide. [Image 4] shows a horizontal alignment figure whose notes reference Table 2C-3 "for advance placement distance guidelines," again characterizing the table as guidelines. Citations: - Table 2C-3 (p.152) - Section 2C.04 Guidance §5 (p.152) - Section 2C.35 Standard §1 (p.177) - Section 8B.06 Standard §3 (p.1001)

**Gold answer:** The table shows 495 feet. It is a guideline to be applied with engineering judgment and may be adjusted for roadway features, other signing, and visibility; it is not an inflexible universal mandate.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: For a curve with a 40‑mph advisory speed and a radius of 401 to 700 feet, Table 2C‑5 indicates a typical Chevron Alignment sign spacing of 120 feet, measured from the point of curvature (PC). The signs must be installed on the outside of the curve, in line with and at approximately a right angle to approaching traffic, at a minimum mounting height of 4 feet. Standards (mandatory): - "If used, Chevron Alignment signs shall be installed on the outside of a turn or curve, in line with and at approximately a right angle to approaching traffic. Chevron Alignment signs shall be installed at a minimum height of 4 feet, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the traveled way." - "The Chevron Alignment sign shall be a vertical rectangle. No border shall be used on the Chevron Alignment sign." - "Chevron Alignment signs shall not be used to mark obstructions within or adjacent to the roadway, including the beginning of guardrails or barriers, as this is the function of an object marker (see Section 2C.70)." - "Chevron Alignment signs shall not be placed on the far side of a T-intersection facing traffic on the stem approach to warn drivers that a through movement is not physically possible, as this is the function of a Two-Direction (or One-Direction) Large Arrow sign." - "Chevron Alignment signs directing traffic to the right shall not be used in the central island of a roundabout or a neighborhood traffic circle." Guidance (recommended): - The approximate spacing of Chevron Alignment signs on the turn or curve, measured from the point of curvature (PC), should be as shown in Table 2C-5; for an advisory speed of 35 to 45 mph and a curve radius of 401 to 700 feet, the sign spacing is 120 feet. - The Chevron Alignment signs should be visible for a sufficient distance to provide the road user with adequate time to react to the change in alignment. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Table 2C-5 shows the row "35 to 45 mph / 401 to 700 feet / 120 feet," and its note cautions that the relationship between curve radius and advisory speed in the table should not be used to determine the advisory speed. - [Image 2] Page 199 shows Section 2C.08 in full, including the Standard placement provisions (outside of curve, right angle to traffic, 4-foot minimum height) alongside Table 2C-5. - [Image 3] Page 197 (Figure 2C-2) illustrates W1-8 chevrons placed on the outside of a curve facing approaching traffic, with a note to see Table 2C-5 for spacing of W1-8 signs. - [Image 4] Page 195 lists Chevron Alignment (W1-8) signs among treatments for changes in horizontal alignment (see Section 2C.08). - [Image 5] Page 660 (Figure 3G-1) notes that Chevron Alignment signs (W1-8) may be used instead of or in addition to standard delineators on a curve. Options (permitted): - Chevron Alignment signs may be used instead of or in addition to standard delineators (per the note on Figure 3G-1, [Image 5]). Citations: - Table 2C-5 (p.158) - Section 2C.08 Standard §4 (p.158) - Section 2C.08 Standard §3 (p.158) - Section 2C.08 Standard §9 (p.158) - Section 2C.08 Standard §10 (p.158) - Section 2C.08 Standard §11 (p.158) - Page 199 - Page 197 - Page 195 - Page 660

**Gold answer:** Typical spacing is 120 feet. The signs must be on the outside of the curve, approximately at right angles to approaching traffic, with a minimum mounting height of 4 feet.

The evidence provided contains no provision addressing what happens when the next no-passing zone begins less than 400 feet later, so that part of the question cannot be answered from this evidence. Standards (mandatory): - "On roadways with center line markings, no-passing zone markings shall be used at horizontal or vertical curves where the passing sight distance is less than the minimum shown in Table 3B-1 for the 85th-percentile speed or the speed limit." - "On two-way, two-lane or three-lane roadways where center line markings are installed, no-passing zones shall be established at vertical and horizontal curves and other locations where an engineering study indicates that passing must be prohibited because of inadequate sight distances or other special conditions." - "When used, the NO PASSING ZONE sign shall be installed on the left-hand side of the roadway at the beginning of no-passing zones identified by pavement markings or DO NOT PASS signs or both." Citations: - Section 3B.03 Standard §4 (p.543) - Section 3B.03 Standard §3 (p.543) - Section 2C.53 Standard §1 (p.191)

**Gold answer:** The no-passing zone must be marked when available passing sight distance is less than 900 feet. If successive no-passing zones are separated by less than 400 feet, the markings should connect them.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: For Warrant 1, Condition A (Minimum Vehicular Volume) with 2 or more major-street lanes and 1 minor-street lane, Table 4C-1 requires 600 vehicles per hour on the major street (total of both approaches) and 150 vehicles per hour on the more critical minor-street approach (one direction only) at the basic 100% level, existing for each of any 8 hours of an average day. The major-street and minor-street volumes must occur during the same 8 hours. Meeting these volumes does not compel signal installation — the warrant only means the need for a signal "should be considered" (Guidance) based on an engineering study; warrants are thresholds for study, not mandates to install. Standards (mandatory): - "These major-street and minor-street volumes shall be for the same 8 hours for each condition; however, the 8 hours that are selected for the Condition A analysis shall not be required to be the same 8 hours that are selected for the Condition B analysis." - "These major-street and minor-street volumes shall be for the same 8 hours for each condition; however, the 8 hours satisfied in Condition A shall not be required to be the same 8 hours satisfied in Condition B." - Table 4C-1 footnotes define the columns: "a Basic minimum hourly volume; b Used for combination of Conditions A and B after adequate trial of other remedial measures; c May be used when the major-street speed exceeds 40 mph or in an isolated community with a population of less than 10,000; d May be used for combination of Conditions A and B after adequate trial of other remedial measures when the major-street speed exceeds 40 mph or in an isolated community with a population of less than 10,000." Guidance (recommended): - Per the Guidance shown on the Section 4C.02 page, the need for a traffic control signal should be considered if an engineering study finds that the 100 percent column volumes of Condition A (or Condition B) in Table 4C-1 exist on the major street and the more critical minor-street approach for each of any 8 hours of an average day — i.e., satisfying the warrant triggers consideration, not a requirement to install. Options (permitted): - The 70% columns (420 vph major / 105 vph minor for this lane combination) may be used in place of the 100% columns when the major-street speed exceeds 40 mph or in an isolated community with a population of less than 10,000. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Table 4C-1, Condition A, row "2 or more / 1": major-street volumes of 600 (100%), 480 (80%), 420 (70%), 336 (56%) vph and minor-street volumes of 150 (100%), 120 (80%), 105 (70%), 84 (56%) vph. - [Image 5] The full Section 4C.02 page shows the Guidance ("The need for a traffic control signal should be considered if an engineering study finds that one of the following conditions exist for each of any 8 hours of an average day") and the introductory text noting that warrants are not the only factor and installation decisions rest on an engineering study. Citations: - Section 4C.02 Standard §5 (p.653) - Section 4C.02 Standard §10 (p.654) - Section 4C.02 Standard §56 (p.654) - Section 4C.02 Standard §42 (p.653) - Table 4C-1 (p.653) - Page 694

**Gold answer:** For each of the same 8 selected hours, 600 vph must exist on the major street (both approaches total) and 150 vph on the critical minor approach (one direction) at the 100-percent thresholds. Meeting the warrant does not compel installation; an engineering study must determine whether a signal is justified.

Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The pedestrian interval sequence is: walk interval (WALKING PERSON), followed immediately by the pedestrian change interval (flashing UPRAISED HAND, typically with countdown), followed by a buffer interval (steady UPRAISED HAND) of at least 2 seconds before conflicting traffic is released. The sum of the pedestrian change interval and buffer interval must be at least the calculated pedestrian clearance time. Clearance timing is normally based on a walking speed of 3.5 feet per second, with slower speeds considered where slower pedestrians or wheelchair users routinely cross, and 3 feet per second used to check the total of walk interval plus clearance time. Standards (mandatory): - "A pedestrian change interval consisting of a flashing UPRAISED HAND (symbolizing DONT WALK) signal indication shall begin immediately following the WALKING PERSON (symbolizing WALK) signal indication. Following the pedestrian change interval, a buffer interval consisting of a steady UPRAISED HAND (symbolizing DONT WALK) signal indication shall be displayed for at least 2 seconds prior to the release of any conflicting vehicular movement. The sum of the time of the pedestrian change interval and the buffer interval shall not be less than the calculated pedestrian clearance time... The buffer interval shall not begin later than the beginning of the red clearance interval, if used." - Where the pedestrian clearance time is sufficient only for crossing to a median, "median-mounted pedestrian signals, with pedestrian detectors... shall be provided and signing such as the R10-3d sign... shall be provided to notify pedestrians to cross only to the median to await the next WALKING PERSON (symbolizing WALK) signal indication." Guidance (recommended): - The pedestrian clearance time should be sufficient to allow a pedestrian who left the curb or edge of pavement at the end of the WALK indication to travel at a walking speed of 3.5 feet per second to at least the far side of the traveled way or to a median of sufficient width to wait. - Where pedestrians who walk slower than 3.5 feet per second, or pedestrians who use wheelchairs, routinely use the crosswalk, a walking speed of less than 3.5 feet per second should be considered. - The total of the walk interval and pedestrian clearance time should allow a pedestrian who left the pedestrian detector (or a point 6 feet behind the curb face/pavement edge) at the start of the WALK indication to travel at 3 feet per second to the far side of the traveled way (or the median in a two-stage crossing); any additional time needed should be added to the walk interval. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 4I-4 shows the interval order — steady WALK (7 seconds minimum), flashing UPRAISED HAND with countdown during the pedestrian change interval, then a steady UPRAISED HAND buffer interval of 2 seconds minimum — with the calculated pedestrian clearance time spanning the change interval and (optionally) the buffer, and the countdown "zero" point at the start of the buffer interval. [Image 2] Page 765 presents Figure 4I-4 in context with the footnotes noting the countdown display is optional for change intervals of 7 seconds or less and that the buffer interval shall always be provided and displayed. [Image 3] Page 764 states the Standard that the change interval begins immediately after the WALK indication, the buffer interval must last at least 2 seconds before release of conflicting traffic, and the sum of the change and buffer intervals must equal or exceed the calculated clearance time. [Image 4] Page 767 addresses related walk-interval timing where a leading pedestrian interval is used. [Image 5] Page 771 concerns pedestrian hybrid beacon operation and is not directly relevant to the interval order question. Citations: - Section 4I.06 Standard §4 (p.724) - Section 4I.06 Standard §16 (p.725) - Section 4I.06 Guidance §7 (p.724) - Section 4I.06 Guidance §10 (p.725) - Section 4I.06 Guidance §14 (p.725) - Figure 4I-4 (p.724) - Page 765 - Page 764

**Gold answer:** The order is WALK, flashing DONT WALK pedestrian change interval, then steady DONT WALK buffer interval. The buffer interval is at least 2 seconds before conflicting traffic is released, and clearance is normally evaluated using 3.5 feet per second.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The pedestrian hybrid beacon sequence is: (1) dark until activated, (2) flashing yellow upon pedestrian actuation, (3) steady yellow, (4) both steady red indications, (5) alternating flashing red, and (6) dark again until the next actuation. The pedestrian walk interval corresponds to stage 4 — both steady CIRCULAR RED signal indications — during which the pedestrian signal heads display the WALKING PERSON (WALK) indication. Standards (mandatory): - "Pedestrian hybrid beacon indications shall be dark (not illuminated) during periods between actuations." - "Following an actuation by a pedestrian, a pedestrian hybrid beacon face shall display a flashing CIRCULAR yellow signal indication, followed by a steady CIRCULAR yellow signal indication, followed by both steady CIRCULAR RED signal indications during the pedestrian walk interval, followed by alternating flashing CIRCULAR RED signal indications during the pedestrian change interval (see Figure 4J-3)." Upon termination of the pedestrian change interval, "the pedestrian hybrid beacon faces shall revert to a dark (not illuminated) condition." - "The pedestrian signal heads shall display a WALKING PERSON (symbolizing WALK) signal indication when the pedestrian hybrid beacon faces are displaying steady CIRCULAR RED signal indications. The pedestrian signal heads shall display a flashing UPRAISED HAND (symbolizing DONT WALK) signal indication when the pedestrian hybrid beacon faces are displaying alternating flashing CIRCULAR RED signal indications." - "The duration of the steady yellow change interval shall be determined using engineering practices in accordance with the provisions in Section 4F.17." - "A pedestrian hybrid beacon face shall consist of three signal sections, with a CIRCULAR YELLOW signal indication centered below two horizontally-aligned CIRCULAR RED signal indications (see Figure 4J-3)." Options (permitted): - Per the note in Figure 4J-3: an optional steady red clearance interval may be added after Interval 3, and an optional short buffer interval (alternating flashing red while the pedestrian signal heads are displaying a steady UPRAISED HAND) may be added after Interval 5. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 4J-3 depicts the six-stage sequence — 1. Dark Until Activated; 2. Flashing Yellow Upon Activation; 3. Steady Yellow; 4. Steady Red During Pedestrian Walk Interval; 5. Alternating Flashing Red During Pedestrian Change Interval; 6. Dark Again Until Activated — explicitly labeling stage 4 as the pedestrian walk interval. - [Image 2] Page 770 shows Figure 4J-3 in page context along with related Section 4J.02 provisions and the start of Section 4J.03 operation standards. - [Image 3] Page 768 provides Section 4J.01 application provisions and Figure 4J-1 installation guidelines for low-speed roadways (context only). - [Image 4] Page 769 provides Figure 4J-2 and the Section 4J.02 design standard requiring the three-section beacon face configuration shown in Figure 4J-3. - [Image 5] Page 759 shows Chapter 4I pedestrian signal head indications (WALKING PERSON and UPRAISED HAND) that operate in conjunction with the beacon sequence. Citations: - Section 4J.03 Standard §1 (p.729) - Section 4J.03 Standard §2 (p.730) - Section 4J.03 Standard §3 (p.730) - Section 4J.03 Standard §10 (p.730) - Section 4J.02 Standard §2 (p.728) - Section 4J.02 Guidance §4 (p.729) - Figure 4J-3 (p.729)

**Gold answer:** Dark, flashing yellow, steady yellow, both steady red, alternating flashing red, then dark. The both-steady-red stage corresponds to the pedestrian walk interval.

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Level 3 performs all aspects within its ODD, subject to a human fallback request. | An ADS performs the entire dynamic driving task on a sustained basis and the term applies to Levels 3-5. | Level 2 performs steering and acceleration/deceleration but still expects the human to perform all remaining aspects of the task, so it is ADAS rather than ADS. Retrieval context: The top five text chunks did not include a chunk that matched the locked page, section, table, or figure evidence. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=0.00, visual recall=0.00, and visual precision=0.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: An Automated Driving System (ADS) is defined as hardware and software "collectively capable of performing the entire Dynamic Driving Task (DDT) on a sustained basis," a term "used specifically to describe a Level 3, 4, or 5 driving automation system." At Level 2, the system executes only steering and acceleration/deceleration while "the human driver performs all remaining aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task" — it performs only part of the DDT, so it is categorized as an Advanced Driver Assistance System (ADAS), not an ADS. At Level 3, the system provides "sustained performance by an ADS of all aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task within a given ODD," with the human's role reduced to responding to a request to intervene; because the entire DDT is performed by the system, Level 3 crosses into the ADS category in Table 5A-1. Standards (mandatory): - "The following acronyms and abbreviations, when used in this Manual, shall have the following meanings: … 5. ADAS-Advanced Driver Assistance Systems 6. ADS-Automated Driving System … 17. DDT-Dynamic Driving Task …" (establishing the mandatory meanings of the terms compared here). Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Table 5A-1 shows Level 2 grouped under the "Advanced Driver Assistance Systems (ADAS)" Automation Category (human driver performs all remaining aspects of the DDT), while Levels 3–5 are grouped under "Automated Driving System (ADS)" because the ADS performs all aspects of the DDT. - [Image 2] Page 801 contains the Section 5A.03 definition: "Automated Driving System (ADS) – The hardware and software that are collectively capable of performing the entire Dynamic Driving Task (DDT) on a sustained basis … this term is used specifically to describe a Level 3, 4, or 5 driving automation system," and the DDT definition. - [Image 4] Page 800 (Section 5A.01–5A.03) provides the surrounding context for driving automation system terminology drawn from SAE J3016. - [Image 3] Page 877 and [Image 5] Page 104 do not bear on the ADS/Level distinction. Citations: - Table 5A-1 (p.760) - Page 801 - Page 800 - Section 1C.03 Standard §1 (p.31)

**Gold answer:** An ADS performs the entire dynamic driving task on a sustained basis and the term applies to Levels 3-5. Level 2 performs steering and acceleration/deceleration but still expects the human to perform all remaining aspects of the task, so it is ADAS rather than ADS. Level 3 performs all aspects within its ODD, subject to a human fallback request.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: In the direction of travel, a TTC zone is typically divided into four areas: the advance warning area (tells traffic what to expect ahead), the transition area (moves traffic out of its normal path), the activity area (where the work takes place), and the termination area (lets traffic resume normal operations). The activity area itself is comprised of three components: the work space, the traffic space, and the buffer space. These divisions are descriptive (Support), not mandatory requirements. Standards (mandatory): - No Standard provision governs the naming or ordering of these areas; the division into four areas is explanatory (Support) and is applied "as needed, based on engineering judgment." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 6B-1 depicts the four areas in travel order—advance warning area, transition area, activity area (containing the hatched work space, the traffic space allowing traffic to pass through, and lateral/longitudinal buffer spaces), and termination area with a downstream taper. [Image 2] shows the Section 6B.06 text stating the activity area "is comprised of the work space, the traffic space, and the buffer space." [Image 3] shows Section 6B.03, which lists the four areas and refers to Figure 6B-1. Support (explanatory): - "A TTC zone is often divided into four areas as needed, based on engineering judgment: the advance warning area, the transition area, the activity area, and the termination area. Figure 6B-1 illustrates the four areas typically included in a TTC zone." - "The activity area is the section of the highway where the work activity takes place. It is comprised of the work space, the traffic space, and the buffer space." - "The traffic space is the portion of the highway in which road users are routed through the activity area." Citations: - Section 6B.03 Support §1 (p.771) - Section 6B.06 Support §1 (p.773) - Section 6B.06 Support §5 (p.773) - Figure 6B-1 (p.772) - Page 812 - Page 814

**Gold answer:** Advance warning, transition, activity, and termination. The activity area consists of the work space, traffic space, and buffer space.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The STOP/SLOW paddle is the primary and preferred hand-signaling device because it gives road users more positive guidance than red flags; flags are reserved for emergency situations only. To stop traffic, the flagger displays the STOP (R1-1) face of the paddle toward road users with the arm extended and the free hand raised with the palm toward approaching traffic, as illustrated in Figure 6D-1. The paddle must be octagonal, on a rigid handle, and retroreflectorized when used at night. Standards (mandatory): - "The STOP/SLOW paddle (R1-1 and W20-8) shall have an octagonal shape on a rigid handle. When used at night, the STOP/SLOW paddle shall be retroreflectorized." - "If more than eight flashing lights are used, the lights shall be arranged such that they clearly convey the octagonal shape of the STOP face of the paddle and/or the diamond shape of the SLOW face of the paddle." - When a flashlight supplements the paddle at night in an emergency, "the flagger shall hold the flashlight in the left hand, shall hold the paddle or flag in the right hand as shown in Figure 6D-1," and "To inform road users to stop, the flagger shall hold the flashlight with the left arm extended and pointed down toward the ground, and then shall slowly wave the flashlight in front of the body in a slow arc from left to right such that the arc reaches no farther than 45 degrees from vertical." Guidance (recommended): - "The STOP/SLOW paddle (see Figure 6D-1 and Table 6G-1) should be the primary and preferred hand-signaling device because the STOP/SLOW paddle gives road users more positive guidance than red flags." Options (permitted): - "A STOP/STOP or a SLOW/SLOW paddle may be used in certain situations (see Section 6D.05), provided the device meets the size and shape requirements for the STOP/SLOW paddle." [Image 3] Page 826 contains Sections 6D.03–6D.05, including the Guidance that "Use of flags should be limited to emergency situations," reinforcing that the paddle is the preferred device. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The downstream end is normally marked by S5-3 END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT | Approaching traffic first encounters an advance School (S1-1) sign, then the School Speed Limit assembly or S5-1 at the beginning of the reduced zone. | S5-2 END SCHOOL ZONE may be used when both zones end together, with a downstream R2-1 optionally mounted. **Model answer:** Direct Answer: Entering the zone, a School (S1-1) sign must first be installed in advance, followed by the School Speed Limit assembly (or S5-1 School Speed Limit When Flashing sign) placed at or as near as practicable to the point where the reduced school speed limit zone begins. If an optional Reduced School Speed Limit Ahead (S4-5) sign is used, it must be followed by the School Speed Limit sign or assembly. Leaving the zone, the downstream end must be marked with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign (with a limited exception allowing an END SCHOOL ZONE alternative per Paragraph 4). Standards (mandatory): - "A School Speed Limit assembly (see Figure 7B-1) or a School Speed Limit When Flashing (S5-1) sign (see Figure 7B-1) shall be used to indicate the speed limit where a reduced school speed limit zone has been established... The School Speed Limit assembly or School Speed Limit When Flashing sign shall be placed at or as near as practicable to the point where the reduced school speed limit zone begins (see Figures 7B-2 and 7B-4)." - "If a reduced school speed limit zone has been established, a School (S1-1) sign shall be installed in advance (see Table 2C-3 for advance placement guidelines) of the first School Speed Limit sign assembly or S5-1 sign that is encountered in each direction as traffic approaches the reduced school speed limit zone (see Figures 7B-2 and 7B-4)." - "Except as provided in Paragraph 4 of this Section, the downstream end of an authorized and posted reduced school speed limit zone shall be identified with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign (see Figures 7B-1, 7B-2, and 7B-4)." - "If used, the Reduced School Speed Limit Ahead sign shall be followed by a School Speed Limit sign or a School Speed Limit assembly." - "The static School Speed Limit assembly shall consist of a top plaque (S4-3P) with the legend SCHOOL, a Speed Limit (R2-1) sign, and a bottom plaque (S4-1P, S4-2P, S4-4P, or S4-6P) indicating the specific periods of the day and/or days of the week that the special school speed limit is in effect (see Figure 7B-1)." - "If a school zone has been designated under State or local statute, a School (S1-1) sign (see Figure 7B-1) shall be installed to identify the beginning point(s) of the designated school zone (see Figure 7B-2)." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 7B-2 illustrates the full sequence in each direction of travel: an advance School (S1-1) sign (with optional SCHOOL S4-3P plaque), an optional Reduced School Speed Limit Ahead (S4-5) sign, the School Speed Limit assembly (SCHOOL S4-3P plaque, SPEED LIMIT 20 R2-1 sign, and S4-1P time-period plaque) at the zone beginning, and an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign OR END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) sign—each optionally paired with a SPEED LIMIT 45 (R2-1) sign—at the downstream end. - [Image 2] Figure 7B-1 shows the components of the School Speed Limit assembly (S4-3P SCHOOL plaque over an R2-1 sign over an S4-1P, S4-2P, S4-4P, or S4-6P plaque), the S5-1 School Speed Limit When Flashing sign, the S4-5 advance sign, and the S5-2/S5-3 end-of-zone signs. - [Image 3] Page 1019 contains the Section 7B.05 Standard text governing the placement of the assembly, the advance S1-1 sign, and the S5-3 end sign. - [Image 5] Page 1016 is the full page view of Figure 7B-2 showing the example school zone signing layout. - [Image 6] Page 1020 (Figure 7B-4) shows an analogous entering/leaving sequence for a school zone with higher fines for speeding, confirming the same S1-1 → S4-5 → School Speed Limit assembly → S5-3/S5-2 pattern. Citations: - Section 7B.05 Standard §1 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §2 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §3 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §14 (p.980) - Section 7B.05 Standard §7 (p.980) - Section 7B.02 Standard §4 (p.972) - Figure 7B-2 (p.975) - Figure 7B-1 (p.973) - Page 1019 - Page 1016 - Page 1020

**Gold answer:** Approaching traffic first encounters an advance School (S1-1) sign, then the School Speed Limit assembly or S5-1 at the beginning of the reduced zone. The downstream end is normally marked by S5-3 END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT; S5-2 END SCHOOL ZONE may be used when both zones end together, with a downstream R2-1 optionally mounted.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: At a passive grade crossing with multiple tracks, the assembly is arranged top to bottom as: the Crossbuck (R15-1) sign at the top, the inverted-T Number of Tracks (R15-2P) plaque mounted directly below the Crossbuck, and the YIELD (R1-2) or STOP (R1-1) sign below that on the same support (unless placed on a separate support per the exception noted). The Number of Tracks plaque is required whenever there are two or more tracks at the grade crossing. Standards (mandatory): - "The Grade Crossing (R15-1) sign (see Figure 8B-1), commonly identified as the Crossbuck sign, shall be retroreflective white with the words RAILROAD CROSSING in black lettering, mounted as shown in Figure 8B-2." - "If there are two or more tracks at a grade crossing, the number of tracks shall be indicated on a supplemental Number of Tracks (R15-2P) plaque (see Figure 8B-1) of inverted T shape mounted below the Crossbuck sign in the manner shown in Figure 8B-2." - "A Crossbuck Assembly shall consist of a Crossbuck (R15-1) sign, and a Number of Tracks (R15-2P) plaque if two or more tracks are present, that complies with the provisions of Section 8B.03, and either a YIELD (R1-2) or STOP (R1-1) sign installed on the same support, except as provided in Paragraph 10 of this Section. YIELD or STOP signs used at passive grade crossings shall be installed in compliance with the provisions of Section 2B.18, and Figures 8B-2 and 8B-3." - "As a minimum, one Crossbuck sign shall be used on each highway approach to every highway-rail grade crossing, alone or in combination with other traffic control devices." - "On each approach to a highway-rail grade crossing and, if used, on each approach to a highway-LRT grade crossing, the Crossbuck sign shall be installed on the right-hand side of the highway on each approach to the grade crossing." Visual evidence: - [Image 5] Page 1036 (Figure 8B-2, "Crossbuck Assembly with a YIELD or STOP Sign on the Crossbuck Sign Support") shows the vertical order — Crossbuck at top (approximately 9 ft mounting height to sign center), the "3 TRACKS" inverted-T plaque immediately below, and the YIELD or STOP sign below the plaque — along with notes that YIELD/STOP signs are used only at passive crossings and a STOP sign only if an engineering study determines it is appropriate. - [Image 1] Figure 8E-5 (p.1039) illustrates the same top-to-bottom arrangement (Crossbuck, "2 TRACKS" plaque, then YIELD or STOP sign) on a single support at a pathway or sidewalk grade crossing. - [Image 4] Page 1038 shows the same Figure 8E-5 layout within Section 8E.05, confirming the assembly order at pathway/sidewalk crossings. - [Image 3] Page 1080 contains Section 8B.04 provisions confirming the assembly requirements, including that a YIELD sign is the default device unless an engineering study supports a STOP sign. - [Image 2] Page 1041 provides related Section 8B.04 mounting-height standards for YIELD or STOP signs on the Crossbuck support. Citations: - Section 8B.03 Standard §1 (p.993) - Section 8B.03 Standard §6 (p.993) - Section 8B.04 Standard §1 (p.995) - Section 8B.03 Standard §3 (p.993) - Section 8B.03 Standard §7 (p.993) - Figure 8E-5 (p.1039) - Page 1036 - Page 1038 - Page 1041 - Page 1080

**Gold answer:** The Crossbuck is at the top, the inverted-T Number of Tracks plaque is directly below it, and the YIELD or STOP sign is below the plaque on the support. The Number of Tracks plaque is required when two or more tracks are present.
