**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A bicycle box must be at least 10 feet long (measured from the upstream edge nearest the motor-vehicle stop line to the downstream edge nearest the crosswalk or intersection) and must contain at least one bicycle symbol marking. Turns on red are prohibited from the lane where a bicycle box is placed. If green-colored pavement is used, it must cover the full limits of the bicycle box, and green pavement generally is limited to specified bicycle facilities, including bicycle boxes. Standards (mandatory): - "If used, the distance from the upstream edge of the bicycle box that is nearest to the stop line for motor vehicles to the downstream edge of the bicycle box that is nearest the crosswalk or intersection shall be at least 10 feet. At least one bicycle symbol marking (see Figure 9E-12) shall be used in the bicycle box." - "Turns on red shall be prohibited from the lane where a bicycle box is placed." - "If used, green-colored pavement shall be used in the full limits of the bicycle box." - "If used, green-colored pavement shall be limited to: ... E. Bicycle Boxes (see Section 9E.12), ..." - "Where an existing stop line for motor vehicles is relocated upstream to install a new bicycle box, the yellow change and red clearance intervals (see Section 4F.17) shall be recalculated and if necessary, reprogrammed to accommodate the length of the bicycle box." Guidance (recommended): - A bicycle lane should be used on the approach to a bicycle box. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 9E-12 (Sheet 1 of 2) shows a bicycle box across one lane dimensioned "10 ft MIN." between the intersection stop line and the advance stop line, with bicycle symbol markings in the optional green-colored pavement. [Image 2] Figure 9E-12 (Sheet 2 of 2) shows a bicycle box across multiple lanes with optional green-colored pavement covering the full box, bicycle symbols, a "10 ft MIN." dimension, and a NO TURN ON RED (R10-11) sign. [Image 3] Page 1146 presents the Section 9E.12 Standard text stating the 10-foot minimum, the bicycle-symbol requirement, and the turn-on-red prohibition. [Image 4] Page 1147 shows Sheet 1 of Figure 9E-12 with the "10 ft MIN." dimension and the Standard that green pavement shall be used in the full limits of the bicycle box. [Image 5] Page 666 presents Section 3H.06, which limits green-colored pavement to specified bicycle facilities including bicycle boxes. Citations: - Section 9E.12 Standard §6 (p.1105) - Section 9E.12 Standard §9 (p.1105) - Section 9E.12 Standard §13 (p.1106) - Section 9E.12 Standard §7 (p.1105) - Section 3H.06 Standard §2 (p.626) - Section 9E.12 Guidance §4 (p.1105) - Figure 9E-12 (p.1106)

**Gold answer:** The bicycle box must be at least 10 feet long, include at least one bicycle symbol, prohibit turns on red from its lane, and, if green pavement is used, fill the entire limits of the box.

Publicly-owned toll roads, including those under the jurisdiction of a public agency, public authority, or public-private partnership; D. Privately-owned toll roads where the public is allowed to travel without access restriction... and E. Grade crossings of publicly-owned roadways with railroads or light rail transit." - "The MUTCD shall not apply to the following types of facilities: A. Roadways within private gated properties where access to the general public is restricted at all times; B. Grade crossings of privately-owned roadways with railroads; and C. **Gold answer:** No. Applicability is independent of public or private ownership or jurisdiction and independent of whether funding is Federal, State, local, or private.

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: A Standard is mandatory or prohibitive and typically uses 'shall.' Guidance is recommended practice with justified deviations allowed and typically uses 'should.' An Option is per… Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The MUTCD defines its four text headings in Section 1C.01. A Standard is a required, mandatory, or specifically prohibitive practice (typically using "shall"); Guidance is a recommended practice in typical situations with deviations allowed based on engineering judgment or study (typically using "should"); and an Option is a permissive condition carrying no requirement or recommendation (Option statements sometimes modify Standard or Guidance statements). The evidence text is cut off before completing the Option definition and does not include the definition or associated verb for Support, so that portion cannot be fully answered from the evidence provided. Standards (mandatory): - "When used in this Manual, the text headings of Standard, Guidance, Option, and Support shall be defined as follows: A. Standard-a statement of required, mandatory, or specifically prohibitive practice regarding a traffic control device. In limited, location-specific cases, the results of a documented engineering study (see Section 1D.03) might indicate a deviation from one or more requirements of a Standard provision to be appropriate. All Standard statements are labeled, and the text appears in bold type. The verb 'shall' is typically used. The verbs 'should' and 'may' are not used in Standard statements. Standard statements are sometimes modified by Option statements." - "B. Guidance-a statement of recommended practice in typical situations, with deviations allowed if engineering judgment or engineering study (see Section 1D.03) indicates the deviation to be appropriate. All Guidance statements are labeled, and the text appears in unbold italic type. The verb 'should' is typically used. The verbs 'shall' and 'may' are not used in Guidance statements. Guidance statements are sometimes modified by Option statements." - "C. Option-a statement of practice that is a permissive condition and carries no requirement or recommendation. Option statements sometimes contain allowable modifications to a Standard or Guidance statement. All Option statements are labeled..." (the evidence text is truncated at this point; the remainder of the Option definition and the Support definition are not available in the provided evidence). Citations: - Section 1C.01 Standard §1 (p.13)

**Gold answer:** A Standard is mandatory or prohibitive and typically uses 'shall.' Guidance is recommended practice with justified deviations allowed and typically uses 'should.' An Option is permissive and typically uses 'may.' Support is informational and conveys no mandate, recommendation, permission, prohibition, or enforceable condition.

Options (permitted): - The evidence provided does not include the text of Paragraph 13 of Section 1B.03, which contains the limited replacement-in-kind exception; its specific conditions cannot be quoted from the available evidence. **Gold answer:** The replacement must generally be a compliant device. A non-compliant device may be replaced in kind only when engineering judgment finds that a lone compliant device among adjacent non-compliant devices would confuse users and/or the planned replacement schedule for the whole series will achieve timely compliance.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. A traffic control device or application that does not comply with the MUTCD is prohibited from use on any street, highway, bikeway, or site roadway open to public travel until official approval to experiment has first been received from FHWA's Office of Transportation Operations. This flows from the broader requirement that non-adopted devices are prohibited unless the formal processes for interpretations, experiments, MUTCD changes, or interim approvals are followed. Standards (mandatory): - "A traffic control device or application that does not comply with the provisions of this Manual shall not be used on any street, highway, bikeway, or site roadway open to public travel (see definition in Section 1C.02) without first receiving official approval to experiment from the FHWA's Office of Transportation Operations." - "Design, application, and placement of traffic control devices other than those adopted in this Manual shall be prohibited unless the provisions of Sections 1B.04 through 1B.08 are followed regarding official interpretations, experiments, changes to the MUTCD, and interim approvals granted by the FHWA." - Traffic control devices on all streets and highways open to public travel "shall be in substantial conformance with the Standards issued or endorsed by the FHWA," per the U.S. Secretary of Transportation under the Highway Safety Act of 1966. - The request for permission to experiment shall contain specified elements, including a statement of the problem and hypothesis, a description of the proposed change and how it deviates from the Manual, illustrations, supporting data, comparison to compliant devices, a legally-binding public-domain statement, time period and locations, control sites (or justification for not using them), and a detailed research and evaluation plan. - A request involving a new device or new application "shall include... a legally-binding statement certifying that the traffic control device is not protected by a patent, trademark, or copyright... and that the traffic control device is in the public domain," and acknowledging that future proprietary protection will result in removal from the MUTCD, cancellation of interim approval, or cancellation of the authorization for experimentation. - "A traffic control device design or application shall not be eligible for official experimentation (see Section 1B.05) or interim approval (see Section 1B.07) unless it is in the public domain." Citations: - Section 1B.05 Standard §2 (p.6) - Section 1B.03 Standard §8 (p.5) - Section 1B.03 Standard §1 (p.5) - Section 1B.05 Standard §10 (p.8) - Section 1B.05 Standard §16 (p.9) - Section 1D.06 Standard §2 (p.34)

**Gold answer:** No. A non-compliant device or application may not be used on a covered public-travel facility without first receiving official experimentation approval from the FHWA Office of Transportation Operations.

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1B.05, p.9, p.8. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Failure to submit two consecutive semi-annual progress reports terminates the experiment and rescinds FHWA approval, requiring restoration of the sites to MUTCD-compliant conditio… | The final report must be submitted within 3 months after the experiment concludes. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.500. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The experimenting agency must submit a final report within 3 months of the conclusion of an approved experiment; if no final report (or notice of mitigating circumstances) reaches FHWA within 6 months of the end of the experimentation period, the experiment is considered terminated and FHWA's approval is rescinded. Similarly, missing two consecutive semi-annual progress reports results in termination of the experiment and rescission of FHWA's approval, with the site(s) required to be restored to MUTCD-compliant condition within 3 months. Standards (mandatory): - "The experimenting agency shall submit a final report within 3 months of the conclusion of an approved experiment. If a final report is not received by the FHWA's Office of Transportation Operations, and the experimenting agency fails to notify the FHWA of any mitigating circumstances within 6 months of the end of the approved experimentation period, then the experiment shall be considered terminated and shall constitute rescission of the FHWA's approval to the experimenting agency, requiring restoration of the site(s) to a condition that complies with the provisions of this Manual within 3 months." - "The experimenting agency shall submit a semi-annual progress report for any approved experiment even if no work was performed during the previous reporting period. Failure to submit two consecutive progress reports shall result in termination of the experiment and shall constitute rescission of the FHWA's approval to the experimenting agency, requiring restoration of the site(s) to a condition that complies with the provisions of this Manual within 3 months." - "The required semi-annual progress reports shall be submitted throughout the course of an approved experiment in accordance with the following schedule: A. No later than August 1st for the preceding period of January through June; and B. No later than February 1st for the preceding period of July through December." Citations: - Section 1B.05 Standard §14 (p.9) - Section 1B.05 Standard §13 (p.8) - Section 1B.05 Standard §12 (p.8)

**Gold answer:** The final report must be submitted within 3 months after the experiment concludes. Failure to submit two consecutive semi-annual progress reports terminates the experiment and rescinds FHWA approval, requiring restoration of the sites to MUTCD-compliant conditions within 3 months.

The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A new traffic control device (or new application of an existing device) is not eligible for official experimentation or interim approval unless it is in the public domain, meaning all proprietary protections such as patents, trademarks, or copyrights must be expressly abandoned. The requesting agency, the manufacturer/developer, and the supplier must all provide a legally-binding statement certifying the device is in the public domain and acknowledging the consequences if proprietary protection is later established. This public-domain requirement does not extend to individual components used to assemble or manufacture the device. Standards (mandatory): - "A traffic control device design or application shall not be eligible for official experimentation (see Section 1B.05) or interim approval (see Section 1B.07) unless it is in the public domain. Express abandonment of any and all forms of proprietary protection, such as patents, trademarks, or copyrights, related to the design and application of the traffic control device shall satisfy the requirement for the traffic control device to be in the public domain." - A request for experimentation involving a new device or new application "shall include from the agency conducting the experiment, the manufacturer and/or developer of the device, and the supplier of the device, a legally-binding statement certifying that the traffic control device is not protected by a patent, trademark, or copyright in accordance with Section 1D.06, and that the traffic control device is in the public domain and can be used freely in traffic control device design and application without infringement or claim of trade secret misappropriation." The statement shall also acknowledge "that if patent, trademark, or copyright protection is established in the future for the device or application, such action will result in its removal from the MUTCD, cancellation of its interim approval, or cancellation of the authorization for experimentation." - The request for permission to experiment shall contain, among other items, "F. A legally-binding statement that the experimental device or application is in the public domain, in accordance with Paragraph 16 of this Section." - "The requirement for the traffic control device to be in the public domain shall not apply to individual components used in the assembly or manufacture of the traffic control device." Citations: - Section 1D.06 Standard §2 (p.34) - Section 1B.05 Standard §16 (p.9) - Section 1B.05 Standard §10 (p.8) - Section 1D.06 Standard §3 (p.34)

**Gold answer:** The traffic-control message, design, or application must be in the public domain and not protected by patent, trademark, or copyright. Legally binding statements are required from the relevant agency/developer/supplier for experimentation. Protection of internal components or manufacturing methods can still be allowed.

The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Under Section 1D.01, an effective traffic control device should fulfill a need; command attention; convey a clear, simple meaning; command respect from road users; and give adequate time for proper response. These are stated as Guidance (recommended practice), and the Manual advises that design, placement, operation, maintenance, and uniformity be carefully considered to maximize a device's consistency with these five principles. Guidance (recommended): - To be effective, a traffic control device should: (A) fulfill a need; (B) command attention; (C) convey a clear, simple meaning; (D) command respect from road users; and (E) give adequate time for proper response. - Design, placement, operation, maintenance, and uniformity should be carefully considered to maximize the ability of a traffic control device to be consistent with these five principles; vehicle speed and road-user types should be carefully considered as an element governing the design, operation, placement, and location of devices. - The proper use of traffic control devices should provide the road user with the information necessary to safely, efficiently, and lawfully use the streets, highways, pedestrian facilities, and bikeways. - In developing messages for display on changeable message signs (CMS), the provisions of Section 1D.01 should be consulted for the principles of an effective traffic control device. Citations: - Section 1D.01 Guidance §3 (p.31) - Section 1D.01 Guidance §4 (p.31) - Section 1D.01 Guidance §5 (p.31) - Section 2L.04 Guidance §3 (p.513)

**Gold answer:** It should fulfill a need, command attention, convey a clear and simple meaning, command respect from road users, and give adequate time for proper response.

Citations: - Section 1D.05 Standard §2 (p.33) - Section 2D.55 Standard §19 (p.271) - Section 9D.12 Standard §27 (p.1084) - Section 2D.59 Standard §7 (p.279)

**Gold answer:** Fluorescent pink is for incident management, orange is for temporary traffic control, and purple is restricted to vehicles with registered electronic toll collection accounts.

The response identifies: Section 2A.03, Section 2F.12, Section 2G.10, Section 1B.01, Table 2A-2, Figure 2A-1. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Regulatory signs give notice of traffic laws or regulations. | Warning signs give notice of a situation that might not be readily apparent. | Guide signs show route designations, destinations, directions, distances, services, points of interest, or other geographical, recreational, or cultural information. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The MUTCD classifies signs by function into three principal categories: regulatory signs, which give notice of traffic laws or regulations; warning signs, which give notice of situations that might not be readily apparent; and guide signs, which show routes, destinations, directions, distances, services, and other geographical, recreational, or cultural information. These functional classes remain distinct even when messages of different types are combined on one sign, in which case specific color-panel rules apply. Standards (mandatory): - "Signs shall be defined by their function as follows: A. Regulatory signs give notice of traffic laws or regulations. B. Warning signs give notice of a situation that might not be readily apparent. C. Guide signs show route designations, destinations, directions, distances, services, points of interest, and other geographical, recreational, or cultural information." - When functional messages are mixed on a single sign, the functional distinction is preserved by color: "When regulatory messages are incorporated within a guide sign, they shall be on a rectangular panel with a black legend on a white background. When warning messages are incorporated within a guide sign, they shall be on a rectangular panel with a black legend on a yellow background." - The functional classes can also be used in combination as separate signs: "A combination of guide and regulatory signs shall be used in advance of and at the initial entry point and all intermediate entry points from general-purpose lanes or facilities to contiguous, barrier-separated, and buffer-separated preferential lanes where access between the preferential and general-purpose lanes is restricted to designated locations." - The MUTCD itself "shall be recognized as the national standard for all traffic control devices installed on any street, highway, bikeway, or site roadway open to public travel," which is the framework within which these functional classifications apply. Visual evidence: - [Image 2] Figure 2A-1 illustrates both regulatory signs (e.g., No Left Turn, Speed Limit, Weight Limit, STOP) and warning signs (e.g., pedestrian crossing, cross-road symbol) with enhanced conspicuity treatments, showing the visual distinction between the two functional classes. - [Image 4] Figure 2M-1 shows examples of guide signs of the recreational/cultural-interest type (directional signs and assemblies with symbols and arrows), consistent with the guide-sign function of conveying directions and points of interest. - [Image 7] shows Table 2A-2, "Common Uses of Sign Colors," which organizes legend and background colors by functional sign type (Regulatory, Warning, Guide, Information, etc.), reflecting the functional classification scheme. - [Image 1] shows text on managed-lane signing where regulatory signs (e.g., R3-series and toll-related signs) and guide signs are treated as distinct functional categories used together on the same facility. Citations: - Section 2A.03 Standard §1 (p.40) - Section 2F.12 Standard §11 (p.392) - Section 2G.10 Standard §12 (p.422) - Section 1B.01 Standard §1 (p.4) - Figure 2A-1 (p.48) - Figure 2M-1 (p.522) - Page 85

**Gold answer:** Regulatory signs give notice of traffic laws or regulations. Warning signs give notice of a situation that might not be readily apparent. Guide signs show route designations, destinations, directions, distances, services, points of interest, or other geographical, recreational, or cultural information.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Word messages on signs must generally be aligned horizontally and read left to right. Names of places, streets, and highways must use lower-case letters with initial upper-case letters (mixed case), while all other legends must be in upper-case letters unless the Manual provides otherwise for a particular sign or message. These rules also carry over to changeable message signs, which must follow the same design and display principles as other signs. Standards (mandatory): - "Except as otherwise provided in this Manual, all word messages shall be aligned horizontally across a sign, reading left to right." - "The sign lettering for names of places, streets, and highways shall be composed of a combination of lower-case letters with initial upper-case letters. The sign lettering for other legends shall be composed of upper-case letters, unless otherwise provided in this Manual for a particular sign or type of message." - "Except as provided in Section 2A.04, all word messages shall use standard wording as shown in this Manual and in the 'Standard Highway Signs' publication." - "In situations where word messages are necessary other than those provided in this Manual (see Paragraph 15 of this Section), the signs shall be of the same shape and color as standard signs of the same functional type." - "All message displays on CMS, whether for traffic operational, regulatory, warning, or guidance information, or for the other allowable message types as defined in this Chapter, shall follow the same design and display principles found in this Manual used for other traffic control signs, except as provided elsewhere in this Chapter." - State and local highway agencies with permanently-installed or positioned CMS "shall issue and maintain a policy regarding the use and display of all types of messages," defining allowed message types, priority, proper syntax, timing, and other messaging elements consistent with the principles governing traffic control devices and signs. Citations: - Section 2A.08 Standard §1 (p.45) - Section 2A.08 Standard §4 (p.45) - Section 2A.08 Standard §2 (p.45) - Section 2A.04 Standard §11 (p.40) - Section 2L.04 Standard §4 (p.513) - Section 2L.02 Standard §10 (p.511)

**Gold answer:** Word messages generally read horizontally from left to right. Place, street, and highway names use initial upper-case letters with lower-case letters; other legends use upper-case letters unless another MUTCD provision specifies otherwise.

The response identifies: Section 2A.15, Section 6F.02, Section 2N.06, Section 2H.11, Figure 2A-2, Figure 6F-1. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The minimum is 5 feet in rural areas and 7 feet in business, commercial, or residential areas where such activity or obstruction is likely, measured as specified from the near pav… Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Roadside signs in rural areas must be mounted with a minimum height of 5 feet, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement. In business, commercial, or residential areas where parking, bicyclist, or pedestrian movements are likely (or where the sign's view might be obstructed), the minimum height is 7 feet, measured to the top of the curb or, absent a curb, to the elevation of the near edge of the traveled way. The same 5-foot/7-foot requirements apply to temporary traffic control signs in Part 6. Standards (mandatory): - "In rural areas, the minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement, of signs installed at the side of the road shall be 5 feet (see Figure 2A-2)." - "In business, commercial, or residential areas where parking, bicyclist, or pedestrian movements are likely to occur, or where the view of the sign might be obstructed, the minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the top of the curb, or in the absence of curb, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the traveled way, of signs installed at the side of the road shall be 7 feet (see Figure 2A-2)." - For temporary traffic control: "The minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement, of signs installed at the side of the road in rural areas shall be 5 feet (see Figure 6F-1)." - For temporary traffic control: "The minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the top of the curb, or in the absence of curb, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the traveled way, of signs installed at the side of the road in business, commercial, or residential areas where parking or pedestrian movements are likely to occur, or where the view of the sign might be obstructed, shall be 7 feet (see Figure 6F-1)." - Exceptions exist for specific signs: the MAINTAIN TOP SAFE SPEED sign follows the same 5-foot (rural) and 7-foot (urban) heights, while "Reference Location and Intermediate Reference Location signs shall have a minimum mounting height of 4 feet... and shall not be governed by the mounting height requirements prescribed in Section 2A.15." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2A-2 illustrates the 5 ft MIN. height for roadside signs in rural areas (panels A, B, D, E) and the 7 ft MIN. height in business, commercial, or residential areas (panel C), along with lateral offsets and secondary-sign heights. - [Image 2] Figure 6F-1 shows temporary traffic control sign installations with 5 ft MIN. in rural areas (panels A, B) and 7 ft MIN. in business, commercial, or residential areas with and without curb (panels C, D). - [Image 3] This page presents the Section 6F.02 text confirming the 7-foot height for signs in business, commercial, or residential areas and above sidewalks, together with Figure 6F-1 depicting the 5 ft and 7 ft minimums. - [Image 4] This full-page view of Figure 2A-2 shows the 5 ft MIN. rural mounting heights and the 7 ft MIN. height where parking or pedestrian movements are likely to occur. - [Image 5] This page contains the Section 2A.15 Standard text stating the 5-foot rural and 7-foot business/commercial/residential minimum heights, with reference to Figure 2A-2. - [Image 6] Figure 2M-3 shows recreational/cultural interest sign assemblies mounted at 5 ft MIN. in rural areas and 7 ft MIN. in business, commercial, or residential areas, consistent with the Section 2A.15 heights. Citations: - Section 2A.15 Standard §4 (p.58) - Section 2A.15 Standard §5 (p.58) - Section 6F.02 Standard §4 (p.790) - Section 6F.02 Standard §5 (p.790) - Section 2N.06 Standard §5 (p.534) - Section 2H.11 Standard §6 (p.466) - Figure 2A-2 (p.51) - Figure 6F-1 (p.790) - Page 831 - Page 92 - Page 99 - Page 566

**Gold answer:** The minimum is 5 feet in rural areas and 7 feet in business, commercial, or residential areas where such activity or obstruction is likely, measured as specified from the near pavement edge or curb.

Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2A.15, Section 8E.03, Section 2G.03, Section 2G.08, Section 2G.10, Section 2E.20. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: At least 17 feet, except where nearby structures or special constrained structures qualify for the specific exceptions in Section 2A.15. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Overhead signs must provide a vertical clearance of not less than 17 feet, measured to the sign, light fixture, or sign bridge, over the entire width of the pavement and shoulders. A lesser clearance is permitted where nearby structures already have a lower vertical clearance, and where overhead devices are placed above sidewalks, at least 7 feet of clearance to the sidewalk surface is required. Standards (mandatory): - "Overhead signs shall provide a vertical clearance of not less than 17 feet to the sign, light fixture, or sign bridge over the entire width of the pavement and shoulders, except where the structure on which the overhead signs are to be mounted or other structures along the roadway near the sign structure have a lesser vertical clearance." [shown on Page 100 image, Section 2A.15] - "Signs that are post-mounted on a median barrier that overhang any portion of the traveled way shall be mounted with a vertical clearance that complies with that of overhead signs." [shown on Page 100 image, Section 2A.15] - "If overhead traffic control devices are placed above sidewalks, the clearance from the bottom of the device to the sidewalk surface directly under the sign or device shall be at least 7 feet." - "Where lateral clearance is limited, Preferential Lane regulatory signs that are post-mounted on a median barrier and that are wider than 72 inches shall be mounted with a vertical clearance that complies with the provisions of Section 2A.15 for overhead mounting if any portion of the sign extends over the roadway or shoulder." (Parallel requirements apply to Preferential Lane warning signs and guide signs.) Options (permitted): - If the vertical clearance of other structures along the roadway near the sign structure is less than 16 feet, the vertical clearance to an overhead sign structure or support may be as low as 1 foot higher than the vertical clearance of the other structures in order to improve visibility of the overhead signs. [shown on Page 100 image] - In special cases the clearance to overhead signs may be reduced if necessary because of substandard dimensions in tunnels and other major structures such as double-deck bridges. [shown on Page 100 image] Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 2A-2, Panel H ("Overhead sign"), graphically depicts the 17 ft MIN vertical clearance from the pavement to the bottom of the overhead sign, along with the 6 ft MIN lateral offset of the support beyond the shoulder. [Image 2] shows the Section 2A.15 Standard text requiring the 17-foot minimum vertical clearance over the entire width of the pavement and shoulders, together with the associated Options for reduced clearance. [Image 5] shows Sections 2E.19–2E.20 on overhead sign installations, noting that vertical clearance of overhead signs is governed by Section 2A.15 and that overhead sign supports require a 6-foot lateral offset. [Image 3] and [Image 4] show Figure 2D-10 examples of overhead signs at an intersection but do not indicate clearance dimensions. Citations: - Page 100 - Figure 2A-2 (p.51) - Section 8E.03 Standard §7 (p.1036) - Section 2G.03 Standard §15 (p.411) - Section 2G.08 Standard §5 (p.419) - Section 2G.10 Standard §19 (p.422) - Section 2E.20 Standard §1 (p.303) - Section 2A.16 Standard §1 (p.59) - Page 344

**Gold answer:** At least 17 feet, except where nearby structures or special constrained structures qualify for the specific exceptions in Section 2A.15.

**Gold answer:** An ALL-WAY (R1-3P) plaque must be mounted below every STOP sign. Numeric-way plaques such as 2-WAY, 3-WAY, or 4-WAY are not permitted with STOP signs.

Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 3D-1 shows a roundabout with yield control on each approach roadway (YIELD word markings and yield lines at the entrances), with no yield control within the circulatory roadway itself. - [Image 3] shows the text of Section 2B.10, including the Standard that YIELD signs at roundabouts shall control the approach roadways and shall not control the circulatory roadway. - [Image 4] shows Section 2B.51, describing Roundabout Circulation (R6-5P) plaques placed below the YIELD signs on roundabout approaches, consistent with yield control on approaches only. - [Image 5] shows Section 3D.04, noting that a yield line may be used to indicate the point behind which vehicles are required to yield at the entrance to a roundabout. - [Image 2] shows a roundabout figure page consistent with approach-only yield control markings. **Gold answer:** A YIELD sign must control each entering approach roadway. It must not be used to control the circulatory roadway.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The three types of signs shown for an exit-ramp terminal where wrong-way entry could occur are the DO NOT ENTER (R5-1) sign, the WRONG WAY (R5-1a) sign, and ONE WAY (R6-1/R6-2) signs. The text evidence supplied for this question (Specific Service signing, ramp control signals, and cloverleaf guide signing) does not contain the governing exit-ramp wrong-way provision (Section 2B.48), so the answer rests on the visual evidence, which includes the Section 2B.46 standard text and Figure 2B-15. Standards (mandatory): - From the visible Section 2B.46 text [Image 3]: "The DO NOT ENTER (R5-1) sign (see Figure 2B-13) shall be used at the following locations: … B. The intersection of an interchange exit ramp with a crossroad as specified in Section 2B.48 (see Figure 2B-15)." - Note: The six text-based Standard provisions provided (Sections 2J.06, 2J.07, 2J.08, 4P.02, 2G.15, 2E.33) do not address wrong-way signing at exit-ramp terminals and cannot support this answer. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2B-15 illustrates the exit-ramp termination layout with DO NOT ENTER signs facing potential wrong-way entrants, WRONG WAY signs along the exit ramp, and ONE WAY signs at the ramp/crossroad junction (with some installations marked optional). - [Image 2] The full page 112 view confirms Figure 2B-15, "Example of Application of Regulatory Signing and Pavement Markings at an Exit Ramp Termination to Deter Wrong-Way Entry," showing the combined DO NOT ENTER, WRONG WAY, and ONE WAY sign application. - [Image 3] Page 150 (MUTCD page 109) shows Section 2B.46 requiring the DO NOT ENTER sign at "the intersection of an interchange exit ramp with a crossroad as specified in Section 2B.48," and Figure 2B-13 depicting the R5-1 DO NOT ENTER, R5-1a WRONG WAY, and R6-1/R6-2 ONE WAY signs. - [Image 4] The table of contents lists Figure 2B-15 ("Example of Application of Regulatory Signing and Pavement Markings at an Exit Ramp Termination to Deter Wrong-Way Entry") and related figures 2B-13 and 2B-17, corroborating the sign family involved. - [Image 5] Figure 2D-18's note 3 directs readers to "Section 2B.48 and Figure 2B-15 for an example of regulatory signing and pavement markings at an exit ramp termination to deter wrong-way entry," confirming that Section 2B.48 governs this application. Citations: - Figure 2B-15 (p.112) - Page 153 - Page 150 - Page 29 - Page 297

**Gold answer:** At least one ONE WAY sign for each crossroad travel direction, at least one DO NOT ENTER sign near the downstream end of the exit ramp, and at least one WRONG WAY sign on the exit ramp facing wrong-way road users are required.

## T027

**Question:** How should the advance placement of warning signs be determined?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2C.04, Section 6B.04, Section 2C.01, Section 6H.02, Table 2C-3, Table 6B-1. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Warning signs should be placed to provide adequate perception-response time. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 4, which produced MRR=0.250. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.482 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Advance placement of warning signs should be based on providing drivers adequate Perception-Response Time (PRT), using the distances in Table 2C-3 applied with engineering judgment; the use of warning signs itself must be based on an engineering study or engineering judgment. Table 2C-3 gives distances by posted or 85th-percentile speed for Condition A (speed reduction/lane changing in heavy traffic) and Condition B (deceleration to a listed advisory speed). In temporary traffic control zones, signs should be placed in advance of the transition and activity areas at distances varying with roadway type, condition, and speed, extending longer (½ mile or more) on freeways and expressways. Standards (mandatory): - "The use of warning signs shall be based on an engineering study or on engineering judgment." Guidance (recommended): - "Warning signs should be placed so that they provide an adequate PRT. The distances contained in Table 2C-3 should be applied with engineering judgment." - "The effectiveness of the placement of warning signs should be periodically evaluated under both day and night conditions." - In TTC zones, "warning signs should be placed in advance of the transition and activity areas at varying distances depending on roadway type, condition, and posted speed," with the closest sign of a series placed approximately 100 feet for low-speed urban streets to 1,000 feet or more for freeways and expressways (see Table 6B-1). - On freeways and expressways, advance warning sign placement should extend farther—as far as ½ mile or more—because drivers are conditioned to uninterrupted flow. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Table 2C-3 lists advance placement distances by posted or 85th-percentile speed (20–85 mph) for Condition A (speed reduction and lane changing in heavy traffic) and Condition B (deceleration to advisory speeds of 0–80 mph), with footnotes explaining the sign-legibility adjustments, PRT assumptions, deceleration rates, and the 100-foot minimum spacing. - [Image 2] Page 193 shows Section 2C.04, Placement of Warning Signs, containing the guidance that signs should provide adequate PRT and that Table 2C-3 distances should be applied with engineering judgment, together with the full Table 2C-3. - [Image 3] Page 839 shows Sections 6H.01–6H.02, including the guidance on positioning TTC advance warning signs in advance of the transition and activity areas per Table 6B-1. - [Image 4] Page 197 shows Figure 2C-2, an example of curve warning sign placement whose notes direct readers to Table 2C-3 for advance placement distance guidelines. - [Image 5] Page 812 shows Section 6B.04, Advance Warning Area, including the guidance that advance warning placement on freeways and expressways should extend as far as ½ mile or more. Citations: - Section 2C.01 Standard §1 (p.147) - Section 2C.04 Guidance §3 (p.152) - Section 2C.04 Guidance §5 (p.152) - Section 6H.02 Guidance §1 (p.798) - Section 6B.04 Guidance §3 (p.771) - Table 2C-3 (p.152) - Page 193 - Page 839 - Page 197 - Page 812

**Gold answer:** Warning signs should be placed to provide adequate perception-response time. Table 2C-3 is an aid, but its distances should be applied with engineering judgment and can be adjusted for roadway features, other signing, and visibility.

Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2C.08, Table 2C-5, p.158. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: They must be vertical rectangles with no border, installed on the outside of the turn or curve, aligned approximately at a right angle to approaching traffic, and mounted at least… Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=1.000. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: When Chevron Alignment (W1-8) signs are used, they must be a vertical rectangle with no border, installed on the outside of the turn or curve, in line with and at approximately a right angle to approaching traffic. They must be mounted so that the bottom of the sign is at least 4 feet above the elevation of the near edge of the traveled way. Standards (mandatory): - "The Chevron Alignment sign shall be a vertical rectangle. No border shall be used on the Chevron Alignment sign." - "If used, Chevron Alignment signs shall be installed on the outside of a turn or curve, in line with and at approximately a right angle to approaching traffic. Chevron Alignment signs shall be installed at a minimum height of 4 feet, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the traveled way." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Table 2C-5 provides the typical spacing of Chevron Alignment signs on horizontal curves by advisory speed and curve radius (e.g., 40 feet for 15 mph or less up to 200 feet for more than 60 mph), noting the table should not be used to determine the advisory speed. - [Image 2] The full page containing Section 2C.08 shows the Standard provisions on shape, border, outside-of-curve placement, right-angle orientation, and the 4-foot minimum mounting height. Citations: - Section 2C.08 Standard §3 (p.158) - Section 2C.08 Standard §4 (p.158) - Table 2C-5 (p.158)

**Gold answer:** They must be vertical rectangles with no border, installed on the outside of the turn or curve, aligned approximately at a right angle to approaching traffic, and mounted at least 4 feet above the near edge of the traveled way.

Correctness rationale: The response contains part of the correct conclusion, but it also omits or misstates a material requirement. Completeness rationale: The audit found incomplete coverage even though the automated clause comparison did not isolate a single missing sentence. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The response provides some evidence localization, but the audit judged it incomplete, imprecise, or partly incorrect. The response cites: Section 1D.08, Section 2L.01, Section 2L.05, Table 2L-2, Table 2A-2, Page 516. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: It should contain no more than four words | A unit of information is one answer to one question that a driver can use to make a decision. | total message length should be governed by the number of information units and the size of the CMS. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.967 and Context Precision@6=0.500. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: In the MUTCD's treatment of changeable message signs, a "unit of information" is illustrated as the answer to a single basic question a road user would ask about a situation — for example, "What happened?" (MAJOR CRASH), "Where?" (AT EXIT 12), "Who is the advisory for?" (drivers heading TO NEW YORK), and "What is advised?" (USE ROUTE 46), each counting as one information unit. The evidence provided does not contain the specific maximum number of words a unit of information should contain, so I cannot state that limit from this evidence; the evidence does, however, establish related limits on message structure (no more than two phases, no more than three lines per phase). Standards (mandatory): - "Each message shall consist of no more than two phases. A phase shall consist of no more than three lines of text. Each phase shall be understood by itself, and the meaning of the entire message shall be the same, regardless of the sequence in which the phases are read. Each line of legend shall be centered on the sign." - "Abbreviations displayed on CMS shall comply with the provisions of Section 1D.08." Guidance (recommended): - Page 516 (visible in [Image 5]) lists design principles for CMS messages, including that no more than three units of information should be displayed in a message phase, no more than four units in a message when traffic operating speeds are 35 mph or more, no more than five units when speeds are less than 35 mph, and that only one unit of information should appear on each line of the CMS. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Table 2L-2 shows an example message broken into four information units, each unit answering one question (What happened? Where? Who is the advisory for? What is advised?) and each counting as 1 information unit, with a note showing how the four units form a two-phase message (MAJOR CRASH / AT EXIT 12 and USE ROUTE 46 / TO NEW YORK). - [Image 2] shows Section 2L.01 (Description of Changeable Message Signs) and 2L.02, providing general CMS provisions but no definition of a unit of information. - [Image 3] shows the table of contents listing Section 2L.05 "Message Length and Units of Information" at page 514. - [Image 4] shows Table 2A-2 on common uses of sign colors for CMS, which is not relevant to units of information. - [Image 5] shows Section 2L.05 guidance principles (items A–G) governing how many units of information should appear per phase, per message, and per line, and notes that Table 2L-2 provides an example of the number of units of information in a message. Citations: - Section 2L.05 Standard §4 (p.515) - Section 2L.05 Standard §1 (p.516) - Section 2L.05 Standard §6 (p.516) - Table 2L-2 (p.515) - Page 557

**Gold answer:** A unit of information is one answer to one question that a driver can use to make a decision. It should contain no more than four words; total message length should be governed by the number of information units and the size of the CMS.

## T031

**Question:** What directional relationship do yellow center line markings and white lane line markings communicate?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 3B.04, Section 3B.05, Section 3B.01, Section 3B.06, Section 3B.07, Figure 3B-2. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Yellow center lines separate lanes traveling in opposite directions. | White lane lines separate lanes traveling in the same direction. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.667. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Color communicates direction of travel: yellow markings separate traffic moving in opposite directions, while white markings separate traffic moving in the same direction. Thus a driver seeing a yellow line on the left knows opposing traffic is on the other side, whereas white lane lines indicate the adjacent lane travels the same way. Reversible lanes use broken double yellow lines because they serve as the center line at some times. Standards (mandatory): - "Center line pavement markings, when used, shall be the pavement markings used to delineate the separation of traffic lanes that have opposite directions of travel on a roadway and shall be yellow." - "When used, lane line pavement markings delineating the separation of traffic lanes that have the same direction of travel shall be white." - On two-lane, two-way roadways, center line markings shall be one of: "Two-direction passing zone markings consisting of a normal width broken yellow line…"; "One-direction no-passing zone markings consisting of a double yellow line, one of which is a normal width broken yellow line and the other is a normal width solid yellow line…"; or "Two-direction no-passing zone markings consisting of two normal width solid yellow lines…" - "Except where a reversible lane (see Section 3B.04) or a two-way left-turn lane (see Section 3B.05) is present, the center line markings on undivided two-way roadways with four or more lanes for moving motor vehicle traffic always available shall be the two-direction no-passing zone markings consisting of normal width double solid yellow lines as shown in Figure 3B-2." - "If reversible lanes are used, the lane line pavement markings on each side of reversible lanes shall consist of a normal width broken double yellow line to delineate the edge of a lane in which the direction of travel is reversed from time to time, such that each of these markings serve as the center line markings of the roadway during some period (see Figure 3B-6)." - "A normal width dotted white line marking shall be used as the lane line to separate a through lane that continues beyond the interchange or intersection from an adjacent deceleration or acceleration lane." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 3B-2 shows multi-lane, two-way roadways where double solid yellow lines separate the opposing directions (arrows pointing opposite ways) while broken white lane lines separate lanes traveling in the same direction. - [Image 2] Figure 3B-3 shows three-lane, two-way applications in which the yellow center line (broken-plus-solid or double solid) divides opposing flows and the white lane line separates the two same-direction lanes. - [Image 3] Figure 3B-1 shows two-lane, two-way applications with broken and solid yellow center lines between opposing arrows, illustrating passing and no-passing zone messages. - [Image 4] Page 582 repeats Figure 3B-3, showing the yellow center line between opposite-direction arrows and the white line between same-direction arrows. - [Image 5] Page 583 repeats Figure 3B-2, with the double yellow center line dividing opposing traffic and white lines dividing same-direction lanes, including left-turn channelization outlined in yellow. - [Image 6] Page 595 (Figure 3B-11, lane-drop markings) shows all-white lane and channelizing lines between same-direction freeway lanes, with yellow only along the left edge of the directional roadway. - [Image 7] Page 674 (Figure 3I-1) shows a two-way street where the yellow center line separates opposing arrows and white lane lines separate same-direction lanes near a crosswalk. Citations: - Section 3B.01 Standard §1 (p.540) - Section 3B.01 Standard §4 (p.540) - Section 3B.01 Standard §6 (p.540) - Section 3B.06 Standard §1 (p.546) - Section 3B.07 Standard §1 (p.548) - Section 3B.04 Standard §1 (p.543) - Figure 3B-2 (p.541) - Figure 3B-3 (p.542) - Figure 3B-1 (p.540) - Page 582 - Page 583 - Page 595 - Page 674

**Gold answer:** Yellow center lines separate lanes traveling in opposite directions. White lane lines separate lanes traveling in the same direction.

## T032

**Question:** When are no-passing-zone markings required at horizontal or vertical curves, and what should be done when successive no-passing zones are separated by less than 400 feet?

**Grade:** C=0.50, Comp=0.50, F=1.00, Cit=0.75, Strict full credit=No

Completeness rationale: The audit found incomplete coverage even though the automated clause comparison did not isolate a single missing sentence. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The response provides some evidence localization, but the audit judged it incomplete, imprecise, or partly incorrect. The response cites: Section 3B.01, Section 3B.12, Section 3B.13, Section 8C.02, Section 3B.03, Section 2C.53. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: They are required on roadways with center line markings where passing sight distance is below the minimum in Table 3B-1 for the relevant speed. | If successive no-passing zones are less than 400 feet apart, the markings should connect the zones. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.833. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: On two-way, two-lane or three-lane roadways with center line markings, no-passing zones must be established at horizontal and vertical curves where an engineering study shows passing must be prohibited, and no-passing zone markings must be used where the passing sight distance falls below the minimum in Table 3B-1 for the 85th-percentile speed or the speed limit. However, the evidence provided contains no provision addressing successive no-passing zones separated by less than 400 feet, so that part of the question cannot be answered from this evidence. Standards (mandatory): - "On two-way, two-lane or three-lane roadways where center line markings are installed, no-passing zones shall be established at vertical and horizontal curves and other locations where an engineering study indicates that passing must be prohibited because of inadequate sight distances or other special conditions." - "On roadways with center line markings, no-passing zone markings shall be used at horizontal or vertical curves where the passing sight distance is less than the minimum shown in Table 3B-1 for the 85th-percentile speed or the speed limit." - "No-passing zones shall be marked by either the one-direction no-passing zone pavement markings or the two-direction no-passing zone pavement markings described in Section 3B.01 and shown in Figures 3B-1 and 3B-3." - "No-passing zone markings shall be used on: A. Two-way roadways at lane-reduction transitions (see Section 3B.12), B. Approaches to obstructions that must be passed on the right (see Section 3B.13), C. Approaches to grade crossings (see Section 8C.02), and D. Approaches to crosswalks." - "When used, the NO PASSING ZONE sign shall be installed on the left-hand side of the roadway at the beginning of no-passing zones identified by pavement markings or DO NOT PASS signs or both." Citations: - Section 3B.03 Standard §3 (p.543) - Section 3B.03 Standard §4 (p.543) - Section 3B.03 Standard §1 (p.542) - Section 3B.03 Standard §2 (p.542) - Section 2C.53 Standard §1 (p.191) Note on the 400-foot question: The evidence supplied does not include any provision concerning successive no-passing zones separated by less than 400 feet. The evidence is insufficient to answer that portion of the question.

**Gold answer:** They are required on roadways with center line markings where passing sight distance is below the minimum in Table 3B-1 for the relevant speed. If successive no-passing zones are less than 400 feet apart, the markings should connect the zones.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Crosswalk markings must be white. Transverse lines, where used, must be between 6 and 24 inches wide (this same 6–24 inch limit applies to the transverse lines that establish a ladder crosswalk). A marked crosswalk must be at least 6 feet wide, increased to a minimum of 8 feet at a non-intersection crosswalk where the posted speed limit is 40 mph or greater. Standards (mandatory): - "Crosswalk markings shall be white. When used, transverse lines shall not be less than 6 inches or greater than 24 inches in width." (Section 3C.03, shown in [Image 2]) - "Except as provided in Paragraph 6 of this Section, the minimum width of a marked crosswalk shall be 6 feet." (Section 3C.03, shown in [Image 2]) - "At a non-intersection crosswalk where the posted speed limit is 40 mph or greater, the minimum width of the crosswalk shall be 8 feet." (Section 3C.03, shown in [Image 2]) - "The transverse lines used to establish the limits of the ladder crosswalk shall not be less than 6 inches or greater than 24 inches in width." - "If paving materials are used to function as the white transverse lines to establish a marked crosswalk, white additives shall be part of the mixture to produce a white surface. The white paving materials shall be retroreflective." (Section 3C.03, shown in [Image 3]) Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 3C-1 shows the dimensional envelopes for each design: transverse lines 6–24 inches wide with a 6 ft minimum crosswalk width; longitudinal bars 12–24 inches wide with 12–60 inch gaps (not exceeding 2.5 times the bar width); ladder crosswalks combining transverse lines (6–24 inches) with longitudinal bars; and bar pairs of 8–12 inch bars separated by a distance d (8 in ≤ d ≤ 12 in), with a footnote that the minimum crosswalk width is 8 feet at non-intersection crosswalks with posted speeds of 40 mph or greater. - [Image 2] Section 3C.03 (Design of Crosswalk Markings) states the white color requirement, the 6–24 inch transverse line width limits, the 6 ft minimum crosswalk width, and the 8 ft minimum at high-speed non-intersection crosswalks, and reproduces Figure 3C-1. - [Image 3] Section 3C.03 continues with the requirement that white paving materials used as transverse lines contain white additives and be retroreflective, and Section 3C.04/3C.05 describe transverse line and high-visibility crosswalk designs. - [Image 4] The table of contents confirms Chapter 3C (Crosswalk Markings) sections, including Section 3C.03 Design of Crosswalk Markings and Section 3C.07 Ladder Crosswalks. - [Image 5] Section 3H.03/3H.04 addresses aesthetic surface treatments for crosswalk interiors, including guidance on contrast gaps adjacent to the white crosswalk lines, but does not alter the white color or dimensional requirements for the crosswalk markings themselves. Citations: - Section 3C.07 Standard §4 (p.592) - Figure 3C-1 (p.590) - Page 631 - Page 632

**Gold answer:** Crosswalk markings must be white. Where transverse lines are used, each line must be at least 6 inches and no more than 24 inches wide. Except for certain ramp-terminal cases, the marked crosswalk should be at least 6 feet wide.

## T035

**Question:** Does satisfying a traffic signal warrant automatically require installation of a traffic control signal?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=0.75, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=No

**Descriptive mean:** 93.8% (Strong partial credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 90%

**Rationale:** The response received 93.8% as the descriptive mean of the four official item scores. The official rubric did not award strict full credit because at least one dimension scored below 1.00. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The response omits or underdevelops these locked elements: it is not conclusive justification. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 4D.11, Section 4C.01, Section 4C.05, Section 4C.06, Section 2B.12, p.651. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The automated gold comparison did not isolate a high-overlap clause. Adjudication note: Correct non-compulsion rule; omits the documented installation case where no warrant is met. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.871 and Context Precision@6=0.500. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. The MUTCD explicitly states that meeting one or more traffic signal warrants does not by itself require installing a traffic control signal. A signal must be justified by an engineering study of traffic conditions, pedestrian characteristics, and physical characteristics of the location, with the warrants serving as factors in that investigation. Standards (mandatory): - "The satisfaction of a traffic signal warrant or warrants shall not in itself require the installation of a traffic control signal." - "Except for a temporary traffic control signal (see Section 4D.11) installed in a temporary traffic control zone, before a traffic control signal is installed at a particular location, an engineering study of traffic conditions, pedestrian characteristics, and physical characteristics of the location shall be performed to determine whether installation of a traffic control signal is justified at that location." - "The investigation of the need for a traffic control signal shall include an analysis of factors related to the existing operation and safety at the study location and the potential to improve these conditions, and the applicable factors contained in the following traffic signal warrants: Warrant 1, Eight-Hour Vehicular Volume; Warrant 2, Four-Hour Vehicular Volume; Warrant 3, Peak Hour; Warrant 4, Pedestrian Volume; Warrant 5, School Crossing; Warrant 6, Coordinated Signal System; Warrant 7, Crash Experience; Warrant 8, Roadway Network; Warrant 9, Intersection Near a Grade Crossing." - If Warrant 4 (Pedestrian Volume) is met and a signal is justified by an engineering study, "the traffic control signal shall be equipped with pedestrian signal heads complying with the provisions set forth in Chapter 4I." - If Warrant 5 (School Crossing) is met and a signal is justified by an engineering study, "the traffic control signal shall be equipped with pedestrian signal heads complying with the provisions set forth in Chapter 4I." - A parallel principle applies to all-way stop control: "The satisfaction of an all-way stop control warrant or warrants shall not in itself require the installation of all-way stop control at an unsignalized intersection." Citations: - Section 4C.01 Standard §3 (p.651) - Section 4C.01 Standard §1 (p.651) - Section 4C.01 Standard §2 (p.651) - Section 4C.05 Standard §6 (p.660) - Section 4C.06 Standard §5 (p.660) - Section 2B.12 Standard §5 (p.79)

**Gold answer:** No. A warrant is a threshold that triggers analysis; it is not conclusive justification. Installation must be based on an engineering study considering safety, operation, alternatives, and the site. A signal can also be installed where no warrant is met if an engineering study documents why it is the best solution.

## T036

**Question:** When a driver faces a steady circular green indication, what movements are generally permitted and to whom must the driver yield?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 4A.03, p.644. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The driver must yield to pedestrians lawfully in an associated crosswalk, vehicles lawfully in the intersection, and for left/U-turns, opposing vehicles close enough to be an imme… | The driver may generally proceed straight, turn right or left, or make a U-turn unless another control modifies the movement. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A driver facing a steady CIRCULAR GREEN indication is permitted to proceed straight through, turn right, turn left, or make a U-turn, unless the movement is restricted by signs, markings, roadway design, separate turn signal indications, or other traffic control devices. The driver must yield to pedestrians lawfully in an associated crosswalk and to other vehicles lawfully within the intersection, and drivers turning left or making a U-turn to the left must additionally yield to closely approaching opposing traffic. Standards (mandatory): - "Vehicular traffic facing a CIRCULAR GREEN signal indication is permitted to proceed straight through or turn right or left or make a U-turn movement except as such movement is modified by lane-use signs, turn prohibition signs, lane markings, roadway design, separate turn signal indications, or other traffic control devices." - "Such vehicular traffic, including vehicles turning right or left or making a U-turn movement, shall yield the right-of-way to: (a) Pedestrians lawfully within an associated crosswalk, and (b) Other vehicles lawfully within the intersection." - "In addition, vehicular traffic turning left or making a U-turn movement to the left shall yield the right-of-way to other vehicles approaching from the opposite direction so closely as to constitute an immediate hazard during the time when such turning vehicle is moving across or within the intersection." Citations: - Section 4A.03 Standard §1 (p.644)

**Gold answer:** The driver may generally proceed straight, turn right or left, or make a U-turn unless another control modifies the movement. The driver must yield to pedestrians lawfully in an associated crosswalk, vehicles lawfully in the intersection, and for left/U-turns, opposing vehicles close enough to be an immediate hazard.

The yellow change interval also may not be shortened or omitted during a transition into preemption control. However, the provided evidence does not describe the function/purpose of the yellow change interval, nor does it address whether a pre-yellow vehicular countdown display may be used — the evidence is insufficient on those two points. Standards (mandatory): - "The duration of the yellow change interval shall be determined using engineering practices." - For emergency-vehicle hybrid beacons: "The duration of the steady yellow change interval shall be determined using engineering practices in accordance with the provisions in Section 4F.17." - For pedestrian hybrid beacons: "The duration of the steady yellow change interval shall be determined using engineering practices in accordance with the provisions in Section 4F.17." - For bicycle signal faces: "The provisions of Section 4F.17 shall apply to the duration of the yellow change and the red clearance intervals of a bicycle signal phase." - During preemption: "During the transition into preemption control, the yellow change interval, and any red clearance interval that follows, shall not be shortened or omitted." - For red/yellow lens automated flagger assistance devices: "A change interval shall be provided as the transition between the display of the flashing CIRCULAR YELLOW indication and the display of the steady CIRCULAR RED indication. During the change interval, the CIRCULAR YELLOW lens shall be steadily illuminated. The gate arm shall remain in the upright position during the display of the steadily illuminated CIRCULAR YELLOW change interval." Citations: - Section 4F.17 Standard §3 (p.708) - Section 4N.03 Standard §4 (p.743) - Section 4J.03 Standard §10 (p.730) - Section 4H.11 Standard §1 (p.717) - Section 4F.19 Standard §3 (p.710) - Section 6L.04 Standard §8 (p.830) Note on insufficiency: The provided evidence does not contain any provision defining the function of the yellow change interval (e.g., warning that the related green movement is ending), and it contains no provision permitting or prohibiting a pre-yellow vehicular countdown display. I cannot answer those parts from this evidence.

**Gold answer:** The yellow change interval warns that permission to proceed or a protected movement is ending. Its duration must be determined using engineering practices, with guidance of 3 to 6 seconds. Pre-yellow displays such as vehicular countdowns or flashing green indications are prohibited, except for permitted advance-warning beacons.

Completeness rationale: The audit found incomplete coverage even though the automated clause comparison did not isolate a single missing sentence. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 4I.04, Section 4I.02, p.720, p.718. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: It is mandatory at a crosswalk when the pedestrian change interval is more than 7 seconds. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.990 and Context Precision@6=0.833. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A countdown display is mandatory whenever a pedestrian signal head serves a crosswalk where the pedestrian change interval is longer than 7 seconds. When used, the countdown must run only during the pedestrian change interval (flashing UPRAISED HAND), never during the walk interval or the red clearance interval of a concurrent vehicular phase, and it must meet specific display and placement requirements. Standards (mandatory): - "All pedestrian signal heads used at crosswalks where the pedestrian change interval is more than 7 seconds shall include a pedestrian change interval countdown display in order to inform pedestrians of the number of seconds remaining in the pedestrian change interval." - "The countdown pedestrian signal shall display the number of seconds remaining until the termination of the pedestrian change interval (flashing UPRAISED HAND). Countdown displays shall not be used during the walk interval. Countdown displays shall not be used during the red clearance interval of a concurrent vehicular phase that is ending simultaneously with or after the end of the pedestrian phase." - "Where countdown pedestrian signals are used, the countdown shall always be displayed simultaneously with the flashing UPRAISED HAND (symbolizing DONT WALK) signal indication displayed for that crosswalk." - "Countdown pedestrian signals shall consist of Portland orange numbers that are at least 6 inches in height on a black opaque background. The countdown pedestrian signal shall be located immediately adjacent to the associated UPRAISED HAND (symbolizing DONT WALK) pedestrian signal head indication." - "The display of the number of remaining seconds shall begin only at the beginning of the pedestrian change interval (flashing UPRAISED HAND). After the countdown displays zero, the display shall remain dark until the beginning of the next countdown." - "All new pedestrian signal head indications shall be displayed within a rectangular background and shall consist of symbolized messages..., except that existing pedestrian signal head indications with lettered or outline style symbol messages shall be permitted to be retained for the remainder of their useful service life." Citations: - Section 4I.04 Standard §1 (p.720) - Section 4I.04 Standard §6 (p.720) - Section 4I.04 Standard §3 (p.720) - Section 4I.04 Standard §4 (p.720) - Section 4I.04 Standard §5 (p.720) - Section 4I.02 Standard §1 (p.718)

**Gold answer:** It is mandatory at a crosswalk when the pedestrian change interval is more than 7 seconds.
