**Model answer:** Direct Answer: After a pedestrian actuation, the pedestrian hybrid beacon must display, in order: a flashing CIRCULAR yellow, then a steady CIRCULAR yellow, then both steady CIRCULAR RED indications during the pedestrian walk interval, then alternating flashing CIRCULAR RED indications during the pedestrian change interval, and finally revert to dark when the change interval ends. The associated pedestrian signal heads must display corresponding WALK and DONT WALK indications keyed to each beacon interval. Standards (mandatory): - "Following an actuation by a pedestrian, a pedestrian hybrid beacon face shall display a flashing CIRCULAR yellow signal indication, followed by a steady CIRCULAR yellow signal indication, followed by both steady CIRCULAR RED signal indications during the pedestrian walk interval, followed by alternating flashing CIRCULAR RED signal indications during the pedestrian change interval (see Figure 4J-3). Upon ... termination of the pedestrian change interval, the pedestrian hybrid beacon faces shall revert to a dark (not illuminated) condition." - "Except as provided in Paragraph 4 of this Section, the pedestrian signal heads shall continue to display a steady UPRAISED HAND (symbolizing DONT WALK) signal indication when the pedestrian hybrid beacon faces are either dark or displaying flashing or steady CIRCULAR yellow signal indications. The pedestrian signal heads shall display a WALKING PERSON (symbolizing WALK) signal indication when the pedestrian hybrid beacon faces are displaying steady CIRCULAR RED signal indications. The pedestrian signal heads shall display a flashing UPRAISED HAND (symbolizing DONT WALK) signal indication when the pedestrian hybrid beacon faces are displaying alternating flashing CIRCULAR RED signal indications. Upon termination of the pedestrian change interval, the pedestrian signal heads shall revert to a steady UPRAISED HAND (symbolizing DONT WALK) signal indication." - "A pedestrian hybrid beacon face shall consist of three signal sections, with a CIRCULAR YELLOW signal indication centered below two horizontally-aligned CIRCULAR RED signal indications (see Figure 4J-3)." (This face design underlies the required sequence.) - The beacon must be actuated to begin the sequence: "The pedestrian hybrid beacon shall be pedestrian actuated." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 4J-3 depicts the six-step sequence: (1) dark until activated, (2) flashing yellow upon activation, (3) steady yellow, (4) steady red during the pedestrian walk interval, (5) alternating flashing red during the pedestrian change interval, and (6) dark again until activated, and notes that an optional steady red clearance interval may be added after Interval 3 and an optional short buffer interval (alternating flashing red while the pedestrian signal heads display a steady UPRAISED HAND) may be added after Interval 5. - [Image 2] Page 729 shows Figure 4J-3 in context with the Section 4J.03 Standard beginning the required sequence following pedestrian actuation. - [Image 3] Page 730 shows the continuation of the Standard requiring the beacon faces to revert to dark upon termination of the pedestrian change interval and specifying the corresponding pedestrian signal head indications. - [Image 4] Page 728 shows Section 4J.02 design requirements, including the three-section beacon face configuration referenced by the sequence in Figure 4J-3. - [Image 5] Page 20 (table of contents) confirms that Chapter 4J covers Pedestrian Hybrid Beacons, with Section 4J.03 addressing their operation. Citations: - Section 4J.03 Standard §2 (p.730) - Section 4J.03 Standard §3 (p.730) - Section 4J.02 Standard §2 (p.728) - Section 4J.02 Standard §3 (p.728) - Figure 4J-3 (p.729)

**Gold answer:** Flashing circular yellow, then steady circular yellow, then both steady circular red during the walk interval, then alternating flashing circular red during the pedestrian change interval, followed by return to dark.

Completeness rationale: The response omits or underdevelops these locked elements: normally it is 0.15 seconds or less and repeats at 1-second intervals when the audible walk indication is not active, subject to stated exceptions. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 4I.05, Section 4K.04, Section 4K.05, Section 4K.01, Section 4H.12, p.734. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: A locator tone must be incorporated | The push button must have a high-contrast vibrotactile arrow on the button, aligned parallel to travel on the associated crosswalk. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Each accessible pedestrian push button must have a vibrotactile arrow on the button itself, with high visual contrast, aligned parallel to the direction of travel on the associated crosswalk, so pedestrians with vision disabilities can identify the correct crosswalk and align themselves. A locator tone is required to help pedestrians find the vibrotactile arrow and push button; the tone must be deactivated during flashing operation (with a specified exception), and if audible beaconing is used, its volume during the pedestrian change interval may be raised only up to 100 dBA from a far-end loudspeaker mounted 7 to 10 feet above the pavement. Standards (mandatory): - "each push button shall clearly indicate by means of a vibrotactile arrow which crosswalk signal is actuated by the push button. Vibrotactile arrows shall be located on the button of the push button assembly, shall have high visual contrast (light on dark or dark on light), and shall be aligned parallel to the direction of travel on the associated crosswalk." - "A locator tone shall be incorporated into the accessible pedestrian signal equipment to help pedestrians with vision disabilities locate the vibrotactile arrow, and the associated push button if a push button is provided." - "Push button locator tones shall be deactivated when the traffic control signal or pedestrian hybrid beacon is operating in a flashing mode. This requirement shall not apply to traffic control signals or pedestrian hybrid beacons that are activated from a flashing or dark mode to a steady (stop-and-go) mode by pedestrian actuations." - "If audible beaconing is used, the volume of the push button locator tone during the pedestrian change interval of the called pedestrian phase shall be increased up to a maximum of 100 dBA, and shall come from a loudspeaker that is mounted at the far end of the crosswalk at a height of 7 to 10 feet above the pavement." - "At accessible pedestrian signal locations where pressing the pedestrian push button is necessary to activate the walk interval, pressing the pedestrian push button shall activate both the walk interval and the accessible pedestrian signals." - "Where used, push buttons intended to be used by both pedestrians and bicyclists shall be located and operated to meet all accessibility requirements (see Section 4I.05)." Citations: - Section 4K.04 Standard §1 (p.734) - Section 4K.04 Standard §2 (p.734) - Section 4K.04 Standard §6 (p.734) - Section 4K.05 Standard §8 (p.735) - Section 4K.01 Standard §14 (p.732) - Section 4H.12 Standard §3 (p.717)

**Gold answer:** The push button must have a high-contrast vibrotactile arrow on the button, aligned parallel to travel on the associated crosswalk. A locator tone must be incorporated; normally it is 0.15 seconds or less and repeats at 1-second intervals when the audible walk indication is not active, subject to stated exceptions.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: RRFBs are normally dark and begin flashing only when actuated by a pedestrian (push button or passive detection), ceasing after a predetermined time or after the pedestrian clears the crosswalk. All RRFB units serving a crosswalk must start and stop flashing simultaneously. The units flash in a rapid, prescribed 800-millisecond sequence at 75 flashing sequences per minute, with no individual indication exceeding 5 flashes per second. Standards (mandatory): - "The RRFB shall be normally dark, shall initiate operation only upon pedestrian actuation, and shall cease operation at a predetermined time after the pedestrian actuation or, with passive detection, after the pedestrian clears the crosswalk." - "All RRFB units associated with a given crosswalk (including those with an advance crossing sign, if used) shall, when activated, simultaneously commence operation of their rapid flashing indications and shall cease operation simultaneously." - "The predetermined flash period shall be immediately initiated each and every time that a pedestrian is detected either through passive detection or as a result of a pedestrian pressing a push button detector, including when pedestrians are detected while the RRFBs are already flashing and when pedestrians are detected immediately after the RRFBs have ceased flashing." - "When activated, the two yellow indications in each RRFB unit shall flash in a rapidly flashing sequence. As a specific exception to the requirements for the flash rate of beacons provided in Paragraph 3 of Section 4S.01, RRFBs shall use a much faster flash rate and shall provide 75 flashing sequences per minute." - During each 800-millisecond flashing sequence, the left and right RRFB indications shall operate using the prescribed sequence: left illuminated ~50 ms; both dark ~50 ms; right illuminated ~50 ms; both dark ~50 ms; left illuminated ~50 ms; both dark ~50 ms; right illuminated ~50 ms; both dark ~50 ms; both illuminated ~50 ms; both dark ~50 ms; both illuminated ~50 ms; both dark ~250 ms. - "The flash rate of each individual RRFB indication, as applied over the full flashing sequence, shall not be more than 5 flashes per second, to avoid frequencies that might cause seizures." Citations: - Section 4L.03 Standard §1 (p.738) - Section 4L.03 Standard §2 (p.738) - Section 4L.03 Standard §5 (p.738) - Section 4L.03 Standard §6 (p.738) - Section 4L.03 Standard §7 (p.739) - Section 4L.03 Standard §8 (p.739)

**Gold answer:** They are normally dark and operate only upon pedestrian actuation, stopping after the predetermined period or after a detected pedestrian clears. All RRFB units for the crosswalk begin and end together. When active, they use 75 rapid-flashing sequences per minute.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The MUTCD establishes—as recommended practice (Guidance, not a mandatory Standard)—that road user and worker safety and accessibility in temporary traffic control (TTC) zones should be an integral and high-priority element of every project, from planning through design and construction. This priority extends to maintenance and utility work, and it explicitly covers motorists, bicyclists, pedestrians (including those with disabilities), and workers. Guidance (recommended): - Road user and worker safety and accessibility in TTC zones should be an integral and high-priority element of every project from planning through design and construction. - Maintenance and utility work should be planned and conducted with the safety and accessibility of all motorists, bicyclists, pedestrians (including those with disabilities), and workers being considered at all times. - If the TTC zone includes a grade crossing, early coordination with the railroad company or light rail transit agency should take place. Citations: - Section 6A.02 Guidance §1 (p.766)

**Gold answer:** Road-user and worker safety and accessibility must be treated as integral, high-priority considerations from planning through design and construction, including motorists, bicyclists, pedestrians with disabilities, workers, and coordination with rail transit where applicable.

An employer-designated person responsible for worker safety must select the appropriate class of garment. Standards (mandatory): - "For daytime and nighttime activity, all workers, including emergency responders, within the right-of-way who are within the TTC zone shall wear high-visibility safety apparel that meets the Performance Class 2 or 3 requirements of the ANSI/ISEA 107-2015 publication entitled 'American National Standard for High-Visibility Safety Apparel and Headwear,' or equivalent revisions, except as provided in Paragraph 4 of this Section. A person designated by the employer to be responsible for worker safety shall make the selection of the appropriate class of garment." - "When uniformed law enforcement personnel are used to direct traffic, to investigate crashes, or to handle lane closures, obstructed roadways, and disasters, high-visibility safety apparel as described in this Section shall be worn by the law enforcement personnel." - "Except as provided in Paragraph 6 of this Section, firefighters or other emergency responders working within the right-of-way shall wear high-visibility safety apparel as described in this Section." Citations: - Section 6C.05 Standard §1 (p.782) - Section 6C.05 Standard §3 (p.782) - Section 6C.05 Standard §5 (p.782)

**Gold answer:** Workers, including emergency responders, within the right-of-way and TTC zone must wear ANSI/ISEA 107-2015 Performance Class 2 or 3 apparel, or an equivalent revision, subject to specified responder exceptions. The employer's designated safety person selects the appropriate class.

Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 6E.01, Section 4O.02, p.787, p.744. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Methods include flaggers, flag transfer, a pilot car, temporary traffic signals, or stop/yield control. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.832 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: When traffic in both directions must use a single lane for a limited distance, movements from each end of the section are required to be coordinated. Alternating one-way movement should be provided by methods such as flagger control, flag transfer, a pilot car, traffic control signals, or stop or yield control. If traffic control signals are used, they must include adequate red clearance intervals and a means (such as interconnection) to prevent conflicting green indications at opposite ends. Standards (mandatory): - "Except as provided in Paragraph 4 of this Section, when traffic in both directions must use a single lane for a limited distance, movements from each end shall be coordinated." - If signals are used: "The provisions of Chapters 4D through 4G shall apply to traffic control signals for one-lane, two-way facilities, except that: A. The durations of the red clearance intervals shall be adequate to clear the one-lane section of conflicting vehicles; and B. Adequate means, such as interconnection, shall be provided to prevent conflicting signal indications, such as green and green, at opposite ends of the section." Guidance (recommended): - "Provisions should be made for alternate one-way movement through the constricted section via methods such as flagger control, a flag transfer, a pilot car, traffic control signals, or stop or yield control." Citations: - Section 6E.01 Standard §1 (p.787) - Section 6E.01 Guidance §2 (p.787) - Section 4O.02 Standard §1 (p.744)

**Gold answer:** Movements from both ends must be coordinated, except for a short, low-volume, mutually visible work zone that can self-regulate. Methods include flaggers, flag transfer, a pilot car, temporary traffic signals, or stop/yield control.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A School Speed Limit assembly (or School Speed Limit When Flashing S5-1 sign) must be placed at, or as near as practicable to, the point where the reduced school speed limit zone begins. A School (S1-1) sign is required in advance of the first School Speed Limit assembly or S5-1 sign encountered in each direction. The downstream end of the zone is normally marked with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign (with an END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) sign permitted as an alternative under Paragraph 4 of Section 7B.05). Standards (mandatory): - "A School Speed Limit assembly (see Figure 7B-1) or a School Speed Limit When Flashing (S5-1) sign (see Figure 7B-1) shall be used to indicate the speed limit where a reduced school speed limit zone has been established based upon an engineering study or where a reduced school speed limit is specified for such areas by statute. The School Speed Limit assembly or School Speed Limit When Flashing sign shall be placed at or as near as practicable to the point where the reduced school speed limit zone begins (see Figures 7B-2 and 7B-4)." - "If a reduced school speed limit zone has been established, a School (S1-1) sign shall be installed in advance (see Table 2C-3 for advance placement guidelines) of the first School Speed Limit sign assembly or S5-1 sign that is encountered in each direction as traffic approaches the reduced school speed limit zone (see Figures 7B-2 and 7B-4)." - "Except as provided in Paragraph 4 of this Section, the downstream end of an authorized and posted reduced school speed limit zone shall be identified with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign (see Figures 7B-1, 7B-2, and 7B-4)." - "At the downstream end of the section to which a particular speed limit applies, a Speed Limit sign showing the next speed limit shall be installed." - "If used, the Reduced School Speed Limit Ahead sign shall be followed by a School Speed Limit sign or a School Speed Limit assembly." - "The speed limit displayed on the Reduced School Speed Limit Ahead sign shall be identical to the speed limit displayed on the subsequent School Speed Limit sign or School Speed Limit assembly." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 7B-2 shows the S1-1 School sign installed in advance of the School Speed Limit assembly (S4-3P/R2-1/S4-1P) at the beginning of the zone, with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) or END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) sign, optionally with an R2-1 sign for the next speed limit (45), at the downstream end. - [Image 2] Figure 7B-4 similarly shows the School Speed Limit assembly at the zone beginning, the S4-5 Reduced School Speed Limit Ahead sign and S1-1 sign in advance, and S5-3 or S5-2 signs paired with an optional R2-1 (45) at the downstream end. - [Image 3] Figure 7B-1 illustrates the components involved: the School Speed Limit assembly (S4-3P SCHOOL plaque, R2-1 sign, and a time-period plaque), the S5-1 School Speed Limit When Flashing sign, the S4-5/S4-5a advance signs, and the S5-3 END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT and S5-2 END SCHOOL ZONE signs. - [Image 4] Page 1019 provides the text of Section 7B.05 Standards §1–§3 governing placement of the assembly, the advance S1-1 sign, and the S5-3 downstream sign, plus the Option (§4) allowing an S5-2 sign instead where the zones end at the same point. - [Image 5] Page 1021 provides Standards §14–§15 on the Reduced School Speed Limit Ahead sign and its required matching speed limit. - [Image 6] Page 1014 shows the Figure 7B-1 sheet identifying the School Speed Limit assembly components and the S5-2/S5-3 end signs. - [Image 7] Page 1013 provides Section 7B.02 context on School (S1-1) sign applications and school zone signing. Citations: - Section 7B.05 Standard §1 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §2 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §3 (p.978) - Section 2B.21 Standard §15 (p.85) - Section 7B.05 Standard §14 (p.980) - Section 7B.05 Standard §15 (p.980) - Figure 7B-2 (p.975) - Figure 7B-4 (p.979) - Figure 7B-1 (p.973) - Page 1019 - Page 1021 - Page 1014 - Page 1013

**Gold answer:** The assembly or S5-1 sign must be at or as near as practicable to the point where the reduced zone begins. A School (S1-1) sign must be placed in advance of the first school-speed-limit sign in each direction. The downstream end is normally marked with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign, with an END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) allowed when the two zones end together.

If installing the box requires relocating an existing stop line upstream, the signal's yellow change and red clearance intervals must be recalculated and reprogrammed if necessary. Green-colored pavement is optional, but if used it must cover the full limits of the bicycle box; bicycle boxes are among the limited applications where green pavement is permitted. Standards (mandatory): - "If used, the distance from the upstream edge of the bicycle box that is nearest to the stop line for motor vehicles to the downstream edge of the bicycle box that is nearest the crosswalk or intersection shall be at least 10 feet. At least one bicycle symbol marking (see Figure 9E-12) shall be used in the bicycle box." - "Where an existing stop line for motor vehicles is relocated upstream to install a new bicycle box, the yellow change and red clearance intervals (see Section 4F.17) shall be recalculated and if necessary, reprogrammed to accommodate the length of the bicycle box." - "If used, green-colored pavement shall be used in the full limits of the bicycle box." - "If used, green-colored pavement shall be limited to: A. Bicycle lanes..., B. Extensions of bicycle lanes through intersections..., C. Extensions of bicycle lanes through areas where motor vehicles enter a mandatory turn lane in which motor vehicles must weave across bicyclists in bicycle lanes..., D. Two-stage bicycle turn boxes..., E. Bicycle Boxes (see Section 9E.12), and F. As a background for bicycle detector symbols..." - "Green-colored pavement shall not be: A. Incorporated into electric-vehicle parking stations or parking stalls, B. Incorporated into crosswalks..., C. Used as a background for shared-lane markings..., or D. Used instead of the required markings for bicycle facilities..." - "If used, the pattern of the green-colored pavement supplementing dotted extension lines shall match the pattern of the dotted lines, thus filling in only the areas that are directly between a pair of dotted line segments." Citations: - Section 9E.12 Standard §6 (p.1105) - Section 9E.12 Standard §7 (p.1105) - Section 9E.12 Standard §13 (p.1106) - Section 3H.06 Standard §2 (p.626) - Section 3H.06 Standard §3 (p.626) - Section 3H.06 Standard §4 (p.626)

**Gold answer:** The box must be at least 10 feet from its upstream edge near the motor-vehicle stop line to its downstream edge near the crosswalk or intersection, must include at least one bicycle symbol, and turns on red must be prohibited from the lane containing the box. If green pavement is used, it must fill the full limits of the bicycle box.

Unless indicated by an asterisk, these abbreviations shall only be used on portable changeable message signs." Guidance (recommended): - "The abbreviations for the words listed in Table 1D-2 that also show a prompt word should not be used on a portable changeable message sign... unless the prompt word shown in Table 1D-2 either precedes or follows the abbreviation, as applicable." For RD WK, the applicable prompt word following the abbreviation is "Ahead" or a "[Distance]". Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows the Table 1D-2 row for "Road Work" with standard abbreviation "RD WK," a dash (no prompt word) preceding the abbreviation, "Ahead, [Distance]" as the prompt word following the abbreviation, and the example "RD WK 1 MILE." [Image 2] presents the same Table 1D-2 (Sheet 1 of 2) on page 36 with the identical Road Work row. [Image 4] (Table 2L-4, Example 6) illustrates a related message-construction example noting that condensing ROAD and WORK into a single word (per Table 1D-2) can accommodate a single-phase message. **Gold answer:** RD WK; it may be followed by AHEAD or a distance, such as 'RD WK 1 MILE.'

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Table 1D-3 lists "DLY" as an unacceptable abbreviation because, although it is intended to mean "Delay," road users commonly misinterpret it as "Daily." The MUTCD prohibits all Table 1D-3 abbreviations for this reason — their potential to be misinterpreted by road users. Standards (mandatory): - "The abbreviations shown in Table 1D-3 shall not be used in connection with traffic control devices because of their potential to be misinterpreted by road users." - By contrast, "When the word messages shown in Table 1D-1 need to be abbreviated in connection with traffic control devices, the abbreviations shown in Table 1D-1 shall be used" — meaning only the approved abbreviations are permitted. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Table 1D-3 (Unacceptable Abbreviations) shows the row "DLY" with the Intended Word "Delay" and the Common Misinterpretation "Daily," directly documenting the misinterpretation risk that motivates the prohibition. Citations: - Section 1D.08 Standard §4 (p.34) - Section 1D.08 Standard §1 (p.34) - Table 1D-3 (p.37)

**Gold answer:** It is intended to mean 'Delay' but can be misinterpreted as 'Daily.'

The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.933 and Context Precision@6=0.500. **Model answer:** Direct Answer: Per Table 2A-1, the octagon is reserved exclusively for the STOP (R1-1) sign, the circle for the Grade Crossing Advance Warning (W10-1) sign, and the pennant (an isosceles triangle with its longer axis horizontal, pointed right) for the NO PASSING ZONE (W14-3) sign. These shapes are mandatory-exclusive: each shape is limited to the indicated sign, and each sign must exclusively use that shape. Standards (mandatory): - "Particular shapes, as shown in Table 2A-1, shall be used exclusively for specific signs or series of signs, unless otherwise provided in this Manual for a particular sign or class of signs." Table 2A-1 lists: "Octagon* — Stop (R1-1)**; Circle* — Grade Crossing Advance Warning (W10-1)**; Pennant (Isosceles Triangle with longer axis horizontal, pointed right)* — No Passing Zone (W14-3)**." - Per the table's footnotes: "* This shape shall be limited exclusively to the sign(s) indicated." and "** This sign shall be exclusively the shape shown." Guidance (recommended): - "Shapes that are exclusive to a particular sign (such as an octagon for STOP, a pennant for NO PASSING ZONE, or a circle for Railroad Advance) should not be obscured by another sign mounted on the back of the same assembly protruding or extending beyond the edge of the sign with the exclusive shape." Recommended alternatives include installing signs on separate mountings, increasing the size of the exclusive-shape sign, or increasing its mounting height. Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Table 2A-1, "Use of Sign Shapes," pairing Octagon with Stop (R1-1), Circle with Grade Crossing Advance Warning (W10-1), and Pennant with No Passing Zone (W14-3), each marked with the exclusivity footnotes (* and **). Citations: - Table 2A-1 (p.42) - Section 2A.05 Standard §1 (p.42) - Section 2A.05 Guidance §3 (p.42)

**Gold answer:** Octagon: STOP; circle: Grade Crossing Advance Warning; pennant: NO PASSING ZONE.

Visual evidence: - [Image 5] Table 2A-2 shows Temporary Traffic Control with a black legend and orange background, Incident Management with a black legend and both orange and fluorescent pink backgrounds, and ETC-Account Only with a black legend and purple background (footnote 5: "The use of purple on signs is restricted per the provisions of Chapter 2F"). - [Image 1] Figure 2F-16 illustrates the purple background of the ETC Account-Only lane canopy sign ("Toll Pass ONLY") in contrast to the green backgrounds of other toll plaza canopy signs. - [Image 3] Figure 6P-12 shows temporary traffic control signs (W20-1, W20-4, W3-3, G20-2) with black legends on orange backgrounds in a work zone application. - [Image 4] Figure 6P-14 likewise shows orange-background temporary traffic control warning signs (W20-1, W20-7, W14-3, G20-2) used at a haul road crossing. **Gold answer:** Temporary traffic control: orange; incident management: fluorescent pink; ETC-account-only: purple.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: For white-on-red signs (e.g., STOP signs), Table 2A-5 specifies a minimum maintained retroreflectivity of W ≥ 35 and R ≥ 7 (in cd/lx/m², measured at an observation angle of 0.2° and an entrance angle of −4.0°), and this single set of values applies across all sheeting types listed. In addition, footnote 4 imposes a minimum sign contrast ratio of ≥ 3:1, computed as white retroreflectivity divided by red retroreflectivity. Agencies must use an assessment or management method designed to keep signs at or above these levels. Standards (mandatory): - "Public agencies or officials having jurisdiction shall use an assessment or management method that is designed to maintain sign retroreflectivity at or above the minimum levels in Table 2A-5." - "Regulatory, warning, and guide signs (see Section 2A.03), and object markers, shall be retroreflective or illuminated to show the same shape and similar color by both day and night, unless otherwise provided in this Manual for a particular sign or group of signs." - "Regulatory signs shall be retroreflective or illuminated (see Section 2A.21)." Guidance (recommended): - Except for signs specifically identified in Paragraph 5 of Section 2A.22, one or more of the methods described in "Maintaining Traffic Sign Retroreflectivity" (FHWA-SA-07-020, Revised 2013), or a method developed based on an engineering study, should be used to maintain sign retroreflectivity at or above the minimum levels in Table 2A-5; signs identified as below the minimum levels should be replaced. Visual evidence: [Image 3] shows Table 2A-5, in which the "White on Red" row reads "W ≥ 35; R ≥ 7" spanning all sheeting-type columns, with the "Additional Criteria" column referencing footnote 4, which states "Minimum sign contrast ratio ≥ 3:1 (white retroreflectivity ÷ red retroreflectivity)"; the table's footnote 1 states the values are in cd/lx/m² measured at an observation angle of 0.2° and an entrance angle of −4.0°. [Image 1] provides the surrounding Section 2A.22 text (Support, Guidance, and Option paragraphs) tied to Table 2A-5. [Image 2] shows page 62 with the Section 2A.22 Standard requiring agencies to maintain retroreflectivity at or above the Table 2A-5 minimums. Citations: - Table 2A-5 (p.62) - Section 2A.22 Standard §2 (p.62) - Section 2A.22 Guidance §4 (p.62) - Section 2A.21 Standard §3 (p.62) - Section 2B.01 Standard §3 (p.67) - Page 104

**Gold answer:** White at least 35 cd/lx/m², red at least 7 cd/lx/m², and a white-to-red contrast ratio of at least 3:1.

## TB020

**Question:** What is the key difference between automation Levels 3 and 4 in Table 5A-1 regarding a human response to a request to intervene?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 5A.03, Table 5A-1, Page 801, p.760. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: At Level 4, the ADS performs the task even if the human driver does not respond appropriately. | At Level 3, the ADS performs the dynamic driving task within its operational design domain with the expectation that the human driver will respond appropriately to a request to in… Retrieval context: The top five text chunks did not include a chunk that matched the locked page, section, table, or figure evidence. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 6, which produced MRR=0.167. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.356 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=0.00, visual recall=0.00, and visual precision=0.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The key difference is the reliance on the human driver as a fallback. At Level 3, the Automated Driving System (ADS) performs the Dynamic Driving Task within a given ODD "with the expectation that the human driver will respond appropriately to a request to intervene." At Level 4, the ADS continues to perform the Dynamic Driving Task "even if a human driver does not respond appropriately to a request to intervene" — that is, the system itself can handle the fallback. Guidance (recommended): (none — the automation level descriptions are informational, not normative) Visual evidence: [Image 1] Table 5A-1 shows Level 3 defined as "The driving mode specific sustained performance by an ADS of all aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task within a given ODD with the expectation that the human driver will respond appropriately to a request to intervene," and Level 4 as the same sustained ADS performance "even if a human driver does not respond appropriately to a request to intervene"; both levels fall under the Automated Driving System (ADS) category. [Image 2] Page 801 reproduces Table 5A-1 along with the Section 5A.03 definitions, confirming that the ADS term "is used specifically to describe a Level 3, 4, or 5 driving automation system." Support (informational): - Section 5A.03 defines the Automated Driving System (ADS) as "The hardware and software that are collectively capable of performing the entire Dynamic Driving Task (DDT) on a sustained basis," a term used specifically for Level 3, 4, or 5 driving automation systems, and states that the Automation Levels are those described in Table 5A-1. Citations: - Table 5A-1 (p.760) - Section 5A.03 Support §1 (p.760) - Page 801

**Gold answer:** At Level 3, the ADS performs the dynamic driving task within its operational design domain with the expectation that the human driver will respond appropriately to a request to intervene. At Level 4, the ADS performs the task even if the human driver does not respond appropriately.
